# Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

## 1. Overview

Shehbaz Sharif was Pakistan’s constitutional prime minister from 11 April 2022 until the National Assembly was dissolved on 9 August 2023. In 2023, his Pakistan Democratic Movement coalition governed through Parliament until August; a caretaker government then took office for the remainder of the year. His formal powers included cabinet leadership, budget and economic policy, federal administration, security coordination, and advice to the president on dissolution.

His practical influence was constrained by a fragile coalition, the courts, provincial governments, the Election Commission, severe fiscal dependence on the IMF and foreign creditors, and—most importantly—the military establishment. The June 2023 Special Investment Facilitation Council formally gave the army chief a role in economic decision-making. The federal government also had limited control over provincial policing and security, particularly in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa and Balochistan.

The year combined an IMF-supported stabilization effort with severe inflation, renewed militant violence, political repression after the 9 May unrest, delayed elections, restrictions on media and internet access, continued flood recovery needs, and the forced-return policy toward undocumented Afghans.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record 1 — Shehbaz Sharif’s authority was shared with powerful unelected institutions

**Finding.** Sharif chaired the federal government, but the 2023 creation of the Special Investment Facilitation Council institutionalized military participation in economic policy. The army chief and military officials received seats alongside civilian officials. This is important because it shows that the prime minister’s formal authority did not translate into exclusive control over major strategic decisions.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan Establishes the Special Investment Facilitation Council,” UNCTAD Investment Policy Monitor, 20 June 2023, [direct link](https://investmentpolicy.unctad.org/investment-policy-monitor/measures/4366/pakistan-establishes-the-special-investment-facilitation-council-sifc/), section describing the council’s establishment and purpose.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Strong institutional connection: the council was established by Sharif’s government and its meetings were chaired by the prime minister.

**Complication.** The government presented the council as a coordination mechanism to reduce red tape and attract investment, not as a transfer of political authority.

**Cautions.** UNCTAD summarizes the measure but does not independently evaluate the informal balance of power. Military influence is partly an inference from institutional design and Pakistan’s broader civil-military structure.

**Independent support.** [Pakistan Board of Investment description](https://invest.gov.pk/node/1996?language_id=en%3Fomfrpegdxlgtkegd); [SIFC official leadership page](https://sifc.gov.pk/leadership_organization); HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*, pp. 5–6.

### Record 2 — The government secured a $3 billion IMF stabilization arrangement

**Finding.** On 12 July 2023, the IMF approved a nine-month, approximately $3 billion Stand-By Arrangement. The program required fiscal adjustment, a market-determined exchange rate, tight monetary policy, and reforms to energy-sector viability, state-owned enterprises, and climate resilience. It provided an immediate disbursement of about $1.2 billion and helped avert an acute balance-of-payments crisis.

**Strongest source.** “IMF Executive Board Approves US$3 billion Stand-By Arrangement for Pakistan,” International Monetary Fund, 12 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/07/12/pr23261-pakistan-imf-exec-board-approves-us3bil-sba), paragraphs 1–2.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Strong. The arrangement supported the federal government’s FY2024 budget and stabilization program.

**Complication.** The arrangement was emergency financing, not evidence that structural reforms had already succeeded. It also imposed adjustment costs through fiscal restraint, exchange-rate liberalization, and energy reforms.

**Cautions.** The IMF describes the program and government commitments from an institutional perspective; it is not an independent welfare assessment.

**Independent support.** [IMF staff-level agreement](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/06/30/pr23251-imf-reaches-staff-level-agreement-with-pakistan-on-a-us-3-billion-stand-by-arrangement); IMF [Pakistan staff report](https://www.elibrary.imf.org/view/journals/002/2023/260/article-A001-en.xml); [World Bank Pakistan overview](https://www.worldbank.org/ext/en/country/pakistan).

### Record 3 — Economic stabilization occurred amid severe hardship and inflation

**Finding.** Pakistan’s stabilization program operated amid very high inflation, low growth, shortages, and falling living standards. HRCP recorded widespread protests over food, fuel, electricity bills, unpaid salaries, layoffs, and low wages. It described inflation as approaching 40 percent and characterized 2023 as a year of widespread economic suffering.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, Human Rights Commission of Pakistan, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–5, lines describing inflation, protests, low growth, and reduced access to services.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with some conditions inherited from the 2022 floods and earlier fiscal crisis.

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate to strong. The federal government controlled the budget, IMF negotiations, energy pricing, and macroeconomic policy, but inflation also reflected global prices, exchange-rate pressures, provincial conditions, and earlier shocks.

**Complication.** The IMF program protected critical social spending and the Benazir Income Support Programme; stabilization may have prevented default and more severe shortages.

**Cautions.** HRCP is an independent rights organization, but its report relies partly on surveys, press reports, official data, and regional correspondents. It explicitly warns that figures may reflect trends rather than the complete picture.

**Independent support.** [World Bank Pakistan economic analysis](https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/endpovertyinsouthasia/pakistan-economic-recovery-inclusive-growth-require-bold-reforms); [IMF Pakistan financing analysis](https://blog-pfm.imf.org/en/pfmblog/2023/10/23/pakistan-financing-conundrum); Pakistan [Economic Survey 2022–23](https://finance.gov.pk/survey/chapters_23/Economic_Survey_2022_23.pdf).

### Record 4 — Political competition was sharply restricted after the 9 May unrest

**Finding.** After Imran Khan’s arrest on 9 May, PTI supporters attacked public and military-linked sites, including the Lahore corps commander’s residence and Radio Pakistan in Peshawar. The government responded with mass arrests, military custody, internet shutdowns, terrorism charges, and plans to try civilians in military courts. HRCP reported intimidation, disappearances, forced political disassociation, and the use of the unrest to reshape the pre-election environment.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6, sections on 9 May, military courts, political engineering, and caretaker governments.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Strong for federal policy and national security decisions, though responsibility was distributed across the cabinet, security agencies, courts, provincial authorities, and the military.

**Complication.** The protests involved genuine violence, arson, attacks on military symbols, and damage to public property. Authorities had a legitimate public-order obligation.

**Cautions.** HRCP is strongly critical of state conduct and notes uncertainty about the number of detainees and legal processes. Its account should be paired with court records, government statements, and reporting on the violence itself.

**Independent support.** [Associated Press report on the political crisis](https://apnews.com/article/59861b8ebb5b4c1eb6ffbbd09828b012); [CPJ report on journalists and protests](https://cpj.org/2023/05/journalists-arrested-and-attacked-media-offices-set-ablaze-amid-pakistan-protests/); [UN Human Rights Committee observations](https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=MgJPU7OeMp3RH32RhKQsM78Xy%2FUmPasiaQqlsVUzVgr9JEXU9cJdv0gxBOOkWUe3LTVyEExaahq4Qd1AdRxomw%3D%3D).

### Record 5 — Media and internet restrictions impaired political information

**Finding.** During and after the May unrest, authorities suspended mobile internet and restricted Facebook, YouTube, and Twitter in parts of Pakistan. Freedom House recorded a four-day nationwide internet shutdown in May and linked digital restrictions to attempts to suppress support for Imran Khan. CPJ documented arrests and attacks involving journalists, including the disappearance or detention of journalist Imran Riaz Khan.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan: Freedom on the Net 2023,” Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-net/2023), introductory findings and sections A3/B1.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Strong at the federal level because telecommunications regulators and the government controlled restrictions, although some actions were implemented by security and regulatory agencies.

**Complication.** The government and security institutions could argue that restrictions were temporary responses to incitement and attacks on public property.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is an advocacy-oriented comparative organization. CPJ’s accounts often rely on journalists, lawyers, and local reports; disappearance cases may remain unresolved.

**Independent support.** [CPJ May 2023 report](https://cpj.org/2023/05/journalists-arrested-and-attacked-media-offices-set-ablaze-amid-pakistan-protests/); [CPJ on the ban on Imran Khan broadcasts](https://cpj.org/2023/03/pakistan-bans-broadcasting-of-ex-pm-imran-khans-speeches-suspends-ary-news-channel/); HRCP, pp. 6–7.

### Record 6 — Militant and insurgent violence increased substantially

**Finding.** CRSS recorded 1,524 violence-related fatalities and 1,463 injuries across 789 terrorist attacks and counterterrorism operations in 2023, a 56 percent increase over 2022. Khyber Pakhtunkhwa and Balochistan accounted for over 90 percent of fatalities and 84 percent of attacks. TTP, Baloch insurgent groups, and Islamic State-linked actors were among the principal threats.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan’s Violence-Related Fatalities Mark a Record 6-Year High,” Centre for Research and Security Studies, 2024, [direct link](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/), paragraphs 29–46.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. Sharif’s cabinet directed federal counterterrorism and security policy, but violence was concentrated in provinces where federal control was limited and where military and provincial authorities played major roles.

**Complication.** CRSS combines terrorism, insurgency, and security operations; it is not a pure measure of attacks against civilians. Some figures differ from CRSS’s own webpage summary and from government totals.

**Cautions.** The dataset depends on media and official reporting in conflict areas. Attribution of attacks and casualties can be contested.

**Independent support.** [CRSS annual report page](https://crss.pk/annual-security-report-2023-pakistan/); [UN Security Council Afghanistan meeting record](https://digitallibrary.un.org/nanna/record/4033247/files/S_PV.9515-EN.pdf?registerDownload=1&version=1&withMetadata=0&withWatermark=0); HRCP, pp. 5–6.

### Record 7 — Flood recovery and essential services remained severely incomplete

**Finding.** One year after the 2022 floods, UNICEF reported that eight million people in affected areas still lacked safe water, more than 1.5 million children needed lifesaving nutrition interventions, and recovery funding was only 57 percent of the agency’s appeal. The floods had affected 33 million people and damaged or destroyed approximately 30,000 schools, 2,000 health facilities, and 4,300 water systems.

**Strongest source.** “One year on from catastrophic floods, millions of children in Pakistan still need urgent support,” UNICEF, 25 August 2023, [direct link](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/press-releases/one-year-catastrophic-floods-millions-children-pakistan-still-need-urgent-support), paragraphs 30–34.

**Period/status.** 2023 condition inherited from the 2022 disaster, with government recovery responsibility continuing during the requested period.

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. Sharif’s federal government led international appeals, reconstruction policy, and national coordination, but implementation depended heavily on provincial governments and donors.

**Complication.** UNICEF and government agencies delivered substantial relief, including health, water, education, and cash assistance; the scale of the disaster exceeded domestic fiscal capacity.

**Cautions.** UNICEF reports humanitarian needs and its own response indicators, not a complete evaluation of federal government performance.

**Independent support.** [UNICEF 2023 consolidated emergency report](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/reports/consolidated-emergency-report-2023); [World Bank report on children after the floods](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099551007112340233/pdf/IDU085b5b1270bae40499408fda0b00c1c3abaf3.pdf); Pakistan’s [Budget 2023–24 speech](https://finance.gov.pk/budget/Budget_2023_24/Speech_english_2023_24.pdf).

### Record 8 — The government ordered a large-scale return of undocumented Afghans

**Finding.** On 3 October 2023, Pakistan’s Apex Committee endorsed a plan to repatriate more than one million undocumented foreigners, most of them Afghans. AP reported a deadline, threatened arrests and expulsions, and more than 2,000 arrests during the initial crackdown. UNHCR warned that forced returns could separate families, deport minors, and expose people to serious protection risks.

**Strongest source.** “UN warns Pakistan that forcibly deporting Afghans could lead to severe human rights violations,” Associated Press, 7 October 2023, [direct link](https://apnews.com/article/pakistan-migrant-crackdown-afghans-human-rights-1a99ea9d909620661d419709831cdced), paragraphs 1924–1959.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns late 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate to strong for the federal government, but the policy was implemented during the caretaker period after Sharif left office. It should not be attributed to Sharif alone.

**Complication.** The government said the policy applied to all undocumented foreigners rather than Afghans specifically and defended it as a sovereign immigration decision. Pakistan also faced serious security and economic pressures connected with the Afghan border.

**Cautions.** AP’s early figures were based partly on official estimates and rapidly changing events. UNHCR’s later figures differ from initial government estimates.

**Independent support.** [UNHCR Pakistan country page](https://www.unhcr.org/where-we-work/countries/pakistan), section on the October 2023 decision; [UNHCR emergency update](https://www.unhcr.org/sites/default/files/2024-01/UNHCR_Pakistan-Afghanistan_Returns_Emergency_Response_Update_8.pdf); [UNHCR December dashboard](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/107878).

### Record 9 — Election timing and caretaker authority became constitutionally disputed

**Finding.** Provincial elections expected after the dissolution of the Punjab and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa assemblies were postponed. The federal National Assembly was dissolved in August, but caretaker governments remained in place beyond the ordinary 90-day expectation. HRCP reported that the Lahore High Court found the Punjab caretaker government had exceeded its mandate by making major policy decisions.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6, sections on postponed elections and caretaker governments.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Connection to ruler.** Strong for the federal coalition’s political environment, but responsibility was shared with the Election Commission, courts, provincial authorities, the president, and later caretaker administrations.

**Complication.** Authorities cited security, financing, administrative, and constitutional difficulties. The political crisis was not created solely by Sharif’s government; provincial assembly dissolutions were initiated by PTI-led provincial governments.

**Cautions.** HRCP’s interpretation is critical of the state and should be paired with court judgments and Election Commission records.

**Independent support.** [UK House of Commons Library briefing](https://commonslibrary.parliament.uk/research-briefings/cbp-9883); [FAFEN publications](https://fafen.org/publications); [Freedom House election analysis](https://freedomhouse.org/article/pakistan-polls-february-election-results-reflect-unexpected-rejection-status-quo).

## 3. Information environment and limitations

Information was available but unusually difficult to interpret.

- The political crisis produced censorship, internet shutdowns, restrictions on broadcasting Imran Khan, arrests of journalists, and intimidation of opposition figures.
- Official data were incomplete or politically contested, particularly for arrests, enforced disappearances, military-court cases, counterterrorism casualties, and Afghan deportations.
- Pakistan’s federal structure complicates attribution: policing, education, health, and much public safety are provincial responsibilities.
- Military influence was substantial but often informal and therefore difficult to document directly.
- Polarization produced sharply conflicting official, opposition, military, and civil-society narratives.
- HRCP explicitly warns that its regional figures may show trends rather than the full picture.
- Humanitarian agencies provide strong service and needs data, but these do not by themselves measure federal policy effectiveness.
- Some candidate URLs were inaccessible or incomplete, including several PDFs and political reports. The records above rely only on sources that were opened sufficiently to extract a specific claim.

## 4. Questions for deeper research

1. What decisions did Sharif personally make during the May crackdown, and which were taken by the military, Interior Ministry, provincial governments, or courts?
2. What were the precise legal bases and chains of command for civilian detention and military-court referrals?
3. Did the IMF program improve reserves and default risk while worsening household welfare, and how did BISP offset those costs?
4. What measurable flood-recovery outputs were delivered by the federal government in 2023?
5. How many Afghans were deported or returned voluntarily during the period, and how many had valid documents or protection claims?
6. What evidence exists of corruption, favoritism, or conflicts of interest involving Sharif, his family, cabinet members, or the SIFC?
7. How much control did the federal government exercise over militant policy in KP and Balochistan?
8. What did the courts, Election Commission, and parliamentary record conclude about election postponement and caretaker powers?

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility: Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister from 11 April 2022 until the National Assembly’s dissolution on 9 August 2023. Under Pakistan’s constitutional system, the prime minister chairs the National Command Authority (NCA), the formal apex body for nuclear policy, force posture, and operational decisions. In practice, nuclear decision-making is heavily institutionalized within the military-led Strategic Plans Division (SPD), with civilian participation but limited public transparency.

The inherited posture in 2023 was an established nuclear arsenal, deliberate doctrinal ambiguity, no declared no-first-use policy, and a declared focus on deterrence against India. Independent technical estimates placed Pakistan’s stockpile at approximately 170 warheads in 2023, with continuing fissile-material and delivery-system expansion. The public record does not show a major nuclear-policy change attributable personally to Sharif during 2023.

Important constraints included the military’s dominance of nuclear institutions, secrecy surrounding arsenal size and command procedures, India–Pakistan strategic competition, Pakistan’s economic crisis, and Sharif’s limited tenure. The principal documented positive developments were continued nuclear-safety cooperation with the IAEA, safeguards over Pakistan’s declared civilian facilities, and public commitments to centralized control and security. The principal unresolved risks were arsenal growth, first-use ambiguity, tactical nuclear capability, incomplete external verification, and limited evidence about actual command-and-control performance.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-001 | Sharif formally chaired the NCA, but nuclear authority was institutionally concentrated in the NCA/SPD system | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.8 |
| WEB-1B-002 | Pakistan’s arsenal was estimated at about 170 warheads and continued expanding | 1B.1, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-003 | Pakistan retained deliberate ambiguity and no declared no-first-use policy | 1B.2, 1B.5, 1B.8 |
| WEB-1B-004 | Pakistan publicly affirmed that its nuclear and missile program was secure and state-controlled | 1B.2, 1B.3 |
| WEB-1B-005 | IAEA safeguards concluded that safeguarded Pakistani nuclear material and facilities remained in peaceful activities in 2023 | 1B.4, 1B.6 |
| WEB-1B-006 | Pakistan maintained a nuclear-safety regulatory and peer-review system through PNRA and the Convention on Nuclear Safety | 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7 |
| WEB-1B-007 | Pakistan expanded international nuclear-security expertise through PNRA’s IAEA collaborating-centre role | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.9 |
| WEB-1B-008 | Nuclear-security and strategic-stability expertise remained largely within military-linked institutions | 1B.3, 1B.7 |
| WEB-1B-009 | Sharif’s public nuclear rhetoric emphasized national capability and economic sovereignty rather than disarmament or de-escalation | 1B.2, 1B.5 |
| WEB-1B-010 | No public evidence located of a 2023 nuclear crisis, test, major deployment change, or nuclear threat by Sharif | 1B.8, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-011 | Pakistan’s nuclear posture remained exposed to India–Pakistan escalation and tactical-nuclear command risks | 1B.5, 1B.8, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-012 | No identifiable 2023 civilian policy framework was located for AI, biological, or other catastrophic dual-use risks | 1B.9 |

### WEB-1B-001 — Formal nuclear authority and practical military control

**Claim.** Sharif formally chaired the NCA during 2023, but the NCA’s secretariat and much of the technical and operational expertise were located in the SPD and military strategic institutions. This means that Sharif possessed formal authority but did not independently control the nuclear system in the ordinary political sense.

**Why it matters.** Attribution should distinguish the prime minister’s legal position from the military’s operational and expert role.

**Strongest source.** “Assessing the security of Pakistan’s nuclear weapon programme,” *Defense & Security Analysis*, 2023, Taylor & Francis, [direct link](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/14751798.2023.2178069), discussion of the 2010 National Command Authority Act and the SPD.

**Period/status.** Inherited institutional condition, applicable during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for formal authority; weak for independent operational control.

**Contrary evidence.** Pakistan’s legal and official framework gives the prime minister and NCA ultimate authority over nuclear policy.

**Cautions.** The source is specialist analysis rather than a publicly available official legal text. Nuclear decision procedures are classified.

**Independent corroboration.** [Arms Control Association, Pakistan’s Evolving Nuclear Doctrine](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine), paragraphs 67–70; [National Command Authority background](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Command_Authority_%28Pakistan%29) — contextual only.

### WEB-1B-002 — Estimated arsenal expansion

**Claim.** The 2023 Nuclear Notebook estimated that Pakistan possessed approximately 170 nuclear warheads and could reach approximately 200 by 2025 at the then-current growth rate. It also identified continuing expansion in delivery systems and fissile-material production.

**Why it matters.** A growing arsenal increases the safety, security, escalation, and command burdens inherited and managed by the government.

**Strongest source.** Hans M. Kristensen, Matt Korda, and Eliana Johns, “Pakistan nuclear weapons, 2023,” *Bulletin of the Atomic Scientists*, 11 September 2023, [direct link](https://thebulletin.org/premium/2023-09/pakistan-nuclear-weapons-2023/), opening assessment and stockpile estimate.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The arsenal was a long-term state and military program, not a policy demonstrably initiated by Sharif.

**Contrary evidence.** Pakistan describes its posture as credible minimum deterrence and defensive, and official public sources do not confirm the independent estimate.

**Cautions.** The estimate relies on open-source intelligence, satellite imagery, and modeling. Pakistan does not publicly disclose its arsenal size.

**Independent corroboration.** [Arms Control Association doctrine analysis](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine); [FAS Nuclear Notebook catalogue entry](https://fas.org/publication/nuclear-notebook-pakistan-nuclear-weapons-2023).

### WEB-1B-003 — First-use ambiguity and tactical-nuclear risk

**Claim.** Pakistan retained a deliberately ambiguous nuclear doctrine and did not endorse a no-first-use policy. Specialist analysis also described Pakistan as maintaining a centralized command system while developing or retaining tactical nuclear options, including the Nasr short-range ballistic missile.

**Why it matters.** First-use ambiguity and tactical nuclear weapons can increase deterrence credibility while also shortening decision timelines and increasing escalation risk.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan’s Evolving Nuclear Doctrine,” Arms Control Association, October 2023, [direct link](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine), paragraphs 66–82.

**Period/status.** Broader inherited posture, documented and applicable in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for creation of the doctrine; moderate for Sharif’s responsibility as formal head of the NCA.

**Contrary evidence.** The same source reports that Pakistan’s weapons are reportedly kept de-mated and under strict centralized control, reducing some accidental or unauthorized-use risks.

**Cautions.** Pakistan’s doctrine is intentionally undeclared. Public interpretations depend on official statements, military commentary, and open-source analysis.

**Independent corroboration.** [Taylor & Francis assessment of Pakistan’s nuclear-security system](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/14751798.2023.2178069); [Arms Control and Proliferation Profile](https://www.armscontrol.org/factsheets/arms-control-and-proliferation-profile-pakistan).

### WEB-1B-004 — Sharif’s public security assurance

**Claim.** In March 2023, the Prime Minister’s Office stated that Pakistan’s nuclear and missile program was a national asset “jealously guarded by the state” and described it as secure and serving its intended purpose.

**Why it matters.** This is direct evidence of the ruler’s public nuclear rhetoric and official representation of control.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan’s nuclear & missile programme totally secure: PMO,” Radio Pakistan, 16 March 2023, [direct link](https://radio.gov.pk/16-03-2023/pakistans-nuclear-missile-programme-totally-secure-foolproof-pmo).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong: the statement was issued by the Prime Minister’s Office.

**Contrary evidence.** Independent sources confirm a robust centralized system but also identify secrecy, doctrinal ambiguity, tactical systems, and continuing arsenal expansion as risks.

**Cautions.** This is a government assurance, not an independent inspection report. “Foolproof” cannot be treated as demonstrated merely because it was asserted.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA safeguards statement for 2023](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/24/06/20240607_sir_2024_part_ab.pdf); [PNRA nuclear-safety report](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/pakistan_cns_national_report_8th_and_9th_rm.pdf).

### WEB-1B-005 — IAEA safeguards over declared civilian facilities

**Claim.** The IAEA reported that, as of 31 December 2023, safeguards were implemented at facilities in Pakistan under safeguards agreements based on INFCIRC/66/Rev.2. The IAEA concluded that the nuclear material, facilities, and other items to which safeguards applied remained in peaceful activities.

**Why it matters.** This is the strongest independent evidence located that Pakistan complied with safeguards obligations for the civilian facilities and materials covered by those agreements.

**Strongest source.** *Safeguards Statement for 2023*, International Atomic Energy Agency, 2024, [PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/24/06/20240607_sir_2024_part_ab.pdf), pp. 8–9, paragraphs 293–304.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The federal government was responsible for treaty implementation, but safeguards administration is conducted through state institutions and technical agencies.

**Contrary evidence.** The conclusion covers only safeguarded material, facilities, and items; it does not certify Pakistan’s military nuclear arsenal or establish that all nuclear activities were subject to comprehensive safeguards.

**Cautions.** Pakistan is not a nuclear-weapon state party to the NPT and does not have full-scope safeguards over its military program. The IAEA’s conclusion is narrower than a general safety certification.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA Pakistan country factsheet](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=PK); [Arms Control Association profile](https://www.armscontrol.org/factsheets/arms-control-and-proliferation-profile-pakistan).

### WEB-1B-006 — Nuclear-safety regulation and international review

**Claim.** Pakistan submitted a national report under the Convention on Nuclear Safety for peer review in 2023. The report described the PNRA’s regulatory framework, safety-culture assessments, workforce measures, Fukushima-related improvements, radioactive-waste policy, and cooperation with the IAEA.

**Why it matters.** It demonstrates the existence of formal civilian nuclear-safety institutions and international review mechanisms.

**Strongest source.** *Convention on Nuclear Safety: National Report for Joint Eighth and Ninth Review Meeting*, Government of Pakistan/PNRA, 2023, [PDF](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/pakistan_cns_national_report_8th_and_9th_rm.pdf), pp. iii, 1–4, 16, and 54.

**Period/status.** Prepared before and reviewed during the 2023 cycle; largely reflects inherited and continuing practice.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The report was submitted on behalf of the government, but technical implementation rested with PNRA and PAEC.

**Contrary evidence.** The report is self-presented and does not independently verify every claimed improvement. It does not address the military weapons program in detail.

**Cautions.** National reports under treaty review are valuable for formal commitments and declared practice but are not equivalent to an adversarial audit.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA 2023 safeguards statement](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/24/06/20240607_sir_2024_part_ab.pdf); [IAEA nuclear-security cooperation with Pakistan](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/news/pakistan-national-institute-of-safety-and-security-becomes-iaea-collaborating-centre-for-nuclear-security-education-training-and-technical-support).

### WEB-1B-007 — Nuclear-security expertise and training

**Claim.** Pakistan’s National Institute of Safety and Security, operated by PNRA, continued an IAEA collaborating-centre agreement covering nuclear-security education, training, technical support, laboratories, expert missions, and fellowships. The arrangement was valid through 2026.

**Why it matters.** It is evidence of institutional investment in nuclear-security expertise and international technical cooperation.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan National Institute of Safety and Security Becomes IAEA Collaborating Centre,” IAEA, 12 October 2022, [direct link](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/news/pakistan-national-institute-of-safety-and-security-becomes-iaea-collaborating-centre-for-nuclear-security-education-training-and-technical-support), paragraphs 5–20 and 23–26.

**Period/status.** Inherited agreement continuing through 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The arrangement predates Sharif’s 2023 period and appears to be a PNRA–IAEA institutional program rather than a personal initiative.

**Contrary evidence.** No evidence was located that Sharif increased funding or personally protected independent dissent within these institutions.

**Cautions.** The source reports the agreement and institutional capacity but does not evaluate performance under crisis conditions.

**Independent corroboration.** [CISS Annual Report 2023](https://ciss.org.pk/PDFs/Annual_Report_2023-web.pdf), pp. 17–19 and 28–30; [PNRA national report](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/pakistan_cns_national_report_8th_and_9th_rm.pdf).

### WEB-1B-008 — Strategic expertise remained military-linked

**Claim.** The 2023 CISS strategic workshop on nuclear command and control, doctrine, safety, security, and export controls included an adviser to the NCA, the SPD director-general for arms control, and the SPD director-general for export controls. This illustrates that Pakistan’s public strategic expertise is closely connected to military and state-security institutions.

**Why it matters.** Expertise exists, but independence and public contestability are difficult to establish.

**Strongest source.** *CISS Annual Report 2023*, Center for International Strategic Studies Islamabad, 2023, [PDF](https://ciss.org.pk/PDFs/Annual_Report_2023-web.pdf), pp. 28–30, especially lines 460–480.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for Sharif personally; moderate for the state’s institutional environment.

**Contrary evidence.** CISS also engaged academics, UNIDIR, the Stimson Center, and international experts, indicating some pluralism and external exchange.

**Cautions.** CISS describes itself as promoting Pakistan’s strategic perspective and should not be treated as an independent evaluator of Pakistan’s nuclear posture.

**Independent corroboration.** [IAEA NISAS collaboration](https://www.iaea.org/newscenter/news/pakistan-national-institute-of-safety-and-security-becomes-iaea-collaborating-centre-for-nuclear-security-education-training-and-technical-support); [Arms Control Association doctrine analysis](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine).

### WEB-1B-009 — Nuclear rhetoric emphasized capability and sovereignty

**Claim.** In a May 2023 statement marking the anniversary of Pakistan’s nuclear tests, Sharif praised the consensus of political, military, and scientific leadership that produced nuclear capability and linked the same national spirit to economic sovereignty and self-reliance.

**Why it matters.** The rhetoric was affirming and nationalist rather than disarmament-oriented. It did not contain an identifiable threat of nuclear use, but it reinforced the prestige and legitimacy of the nuclear program.

**Strongest source.** “President, PM praise scientists, political, military leadership for achievement of nuclear capability,” Radio Pakistan, 28 May 2023, [direct link](https://www.radio.gov.pk/28-05-2023/president-pm-praise-scientists-political-military-leadership-for-achievement-of-nuclear-capability).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong.

**Contrary evidence.** No reckless threat or explicit nuclear brinkmanship was located in the inspected 2023 statements.

**Cautions.** State media reproduces official messaging and is not independent. Absence of a threat in inspected material is not evidence that all rhetoric was responsible.

**Independent corroboration.** [Arms Control Association account of Pakistan’s official and military doctrinal statements](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine), paragraphs 67–84.

### WEB-1B-010 — No documented 2023 nuclear crisis or major posture change

**Claim.** The inspected 2023 record did not identify a Pakistani nuclear test, publicly announced nuclear deployment change, nuclear-use threat, or nuclear crisis decision personally attributable to Sharif.

**Why it matters.** It limits what can reasonably be attributed to the ruler during this short period.

**Strongest source.** The combined 2023 record from [the Nuclear Notebook](https://thebulletin.org/premium/2023-09/pakistan-nuclear-weapons-2023/), [Arms Control Association](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine), [IAEA safeguards reporting](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/24/06/20240607_sir_2024_part_ab.pdf), and Pakistan’s official statements.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, but is a bounded negative finding.

**Ruler attribution.** Not applicable as a positive act; it supports caution against over-attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Pakistan continued arsenal modernization and maintained an escalatory posture based on first-use ambiguity, so “no crisis” does not mean “no risk.”

**Cautions.** Nuclear command decisions are classified. Public silence cannot establish that no relevant changes occurred.

**Independent corroboration.** [CISS Annual Report 2023](https://ciss.org.pk/PDFs/Annual_Report_2023-web.pdf); [Pakistan Nuclear Safety National Report](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/11/pakistan_cns_national_report_8th_and_9th_rm.pdf).

### WEB-1B-011 — Enduring India–Pakistan escalation risk

**Claim.** Pakistan’s nuclear posture remained primarily India-specific, with deliberate ambiguity, no no-first-use commitment, tactical-nuclear capability, and a perceived need to offset India’s conventional superiority. These features preserved deterrence but also created escalation and miscalculation risks.

**Why it matters.** The principal existential-risk exposure during Sharif’s period was not a new 2023 decision but the inherited regional security structure.

**Strongest source.** Arms Control Association, “Pakistan’s Evolving Nuclear Doctrine,” October 2023, [direct link](https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-10/features/pakistans-evolving-nuclear-doctrine), paragraphs 66–92.

**Period/status.** Inherited condition, reviewed in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low for underlying posture; moderate for Sharif’s responsibility to preserve crisis safeguards as NCA chair.

**Contrary evidence.** Pakistan’s reported de-mated weapons and centralized command system are intended to reduce accidental or unauthorized use.

**Cautions.** The article relies partly on official statements and open-source assessments. Actual readiness levels and command procedures are not public.

**Independent corroboration.** [Nuclear weapons security crises study](https://media.defense.gov/2023/Apr/28/2003210847/-1/-1/0/2228.PDF); [Pakistan nuclear-security assessment](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/14751798.2023.2178069).

### WEB-1B-012 — Limited evidence on AI, cyber, biological, and other dual-use risks

**Claim.** The inspected record showed discussion of cyber, AI, autonomy, and emerging technologies in a November 2023 CISS–UNIDIR workshop, but no identifiable Sharif-government law, budget, strategy, regulator, or public review mechanism specifically addressing catastrophic dual-use risks.

**Why it matters.** The evidence supports institutional awareness, not a finding of effective government risk management.

**Strongest source.** *CISS Annual Report 2023*, CISS, 2023, [PDF](https://ciss.org.pk/PDFs/Annual_Report_2023-web.pdf), pp. 12–13, lines 254–268 and pp. 12–13/16, lines 320–335.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low. The workshop was organized by a strategic think tank with UNIDIR, not demonstrably by Sharif’s cabinet.

**Contrary evidence.** The existence of the workshop shows that Pakistani experts were engaging with cyber, AI, and autonomy risks.

**Cautions.** Absence of a located policy document is not proof that no internal policy existed. Public policy documentation in security fields may be classified.

**Independent corroboration.** [ArXiv paper on nuclear verification and AI treaties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2304.04123) — discovery lead only; no ruler attribution established.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk:** **Partially evidenced; unresolved.** No 2023 Sharif initiative to reduce the nuclear arsenal or regional nuclear danger was located. The government maintained existing safeguards and international cooperation, but the arsenal continued expanding.
- **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** **Partially evidenced.** Sharif’s inspected statements were nationalist and capability-affirming but did not contain reckless nuclear threats. Pakistan’s broader doctrine remained deliberately ambiguous.
- **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control:** **Partially evidenced.** Formal centralized command, PNRA regulation, IAEA cooperation, and security training are documented. Independent evidence about military custody, resources, inspections, and failure correction is limited.
- **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections:** **Partially evidenced.** IAEA safeguards operated over declared civilian facilities and Pakistan participated in Convention on Nuclear Safety processes. Pakistan’s military facilities were not subject to comprehensive safeguards, and it remained outside the NPT.
- **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression:** **No direct 2023 evidence of Sharif using nuclear weapons to shield aggression or repression.** The inherited posture’s first-use ambiguity and India-specific deterrence remain relevant background risks.
- **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation:** **Insufficient direct 2023 evidence.** Pakistan maintained export-control and strategic-planning institutions, but no 2023 inspection, enforcement action, or proliferation investigation tied to Sharif was located.
- **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise:** **Mixed.** Pakistan clearly maintained technical expertise and international training, but much strategic expertise was embedded in military-linked institutions. Independent dissent and access cannot be assessed confidently.
- **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation:** **Insufficient direct evidence.** No nuclear crisis decision or de-escalation episode involving Sharif was identified in 2023.
- **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks:** **Insufficient evidence.** AI, cyber, and autonomy risks were discussed by expert institutions, but no clearly attributable government safeguards were found.
- **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture:** **Unresolved.** Pakistan ended 2023 with functioning safeguards and continued safety cooperation, but no demonstrably safer nuclear posture, arsenal reduction, doctrinal clarification, or durable de-escalation measure was identified.

## 5. Counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected or attempted: 15.
- Final evidence records: 12.
- Main independent source families retained: IAEA, Arms Control Association, Taylor & Francis scholarship, CISS, Pakistan government/PMO, and Radio Pakistan.
- Major unresolved gaps: actual NCA meeting records, nuclear-security budgets and staffing, military command-and-control procedures, export-control enforcement cases, proliferation investigations, nuclear-crisis communications, and any classified or internal changes to posture during 2023.
- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security: Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

## 1. Orientation

### Conflict-exposure gate

Pakistan faced three relevant international-security exposures in 2023:

1. An inherited India–Pakistan conflict centered on Kashmir, with a Line of Control ceasefire that largely held.
2. A worsening Pakistan–Afghanistan security dispute involving TTP attacks, alleged militant sanctuaries in Afghanistan, border pressure, and refugee policy.
3. Internal militant violence by TTP, BLA, ISKP, and affiliated groups, sometimes with alleged cross-border links.

There was no conventional interstate war between Pakistan and India in 2023. The principal international-security deterioration concerned Afghanistan and militant violence, while the India–Pakistan front remained comparatively restrained.

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister until 9 August 2023. He chaired the federal cabinet and National Security Committee and exercised formal authority over foreign and security policy. His practical authority was constrained by the military establishment, which retained decisive influence over Afghanistan, India, Kashmir, counterterrorism, and border policy. After August, a caretaker government governed; late-2023 measures, especially the Afghan deportation campaign, should not be attributed to Sharif alone.

The strongest evidence of restraint was Sharif’s January call for dialogue with India and the continued Line of Control ceasefire. The strongest adverse evidence was the government’s increasingly coercive approach toward Afghanistan and the lack of transparent accountability for counterterrorism abuses and civilian harm. The 2023 record does not establish that Sharif initiated a war of choice or deliberately prolonged an interstate war.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-001 | Sharif publicly called for serious talks with India and warned against war | 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.7 |
| WEB-2B-002 | The India–Pakistan Line of Control ceasefire largely held in 2023 | 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-003 | Sharif used SCO diplomacy to seek regional cooperation on Afghanistan and terrorism | 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-004 | Pakistan’s security crisis worsened, with substantial militant violence and attacks on civilians and security forces | 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.8 |
| WEB-2B-005 | Pakistan demanded that Afghanistan stop permitting militant use of its territory | 2B.1, 2B.3 |
| WEB-2B-006 | The Afghan dispute involved competing claims and limited independent access to affected areas | 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-007 | The Afghan deportation policy created protection risks and was implemented mainly after Sharif left office | 2B.4, 2B.5, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-008 | Counterterrorism operations and law-enforcement conduct included alleged unlawful killings, disappearances, and weak accountability | 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-009 | Pakistan’s military resources addressed genuine security threats but were not transparently linked to proportionality or civilian oversight | 2B.8 |
| WEB-2B-010 | The May 2023 political violence and military trials weakened lawful security oversight, though this was primarily a domestic 3B issue | 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-011 | Pakistan’s official foreign-policy rhetoric combined support for peaceful settlement with continued maximalist Kashmir conditions | 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.7 |
| WEB-2B-012 | No durable international settlement or materially safer regional-security institution was created during the period | 2B.7, 2B.10 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-2B-001 — Sharif called for dialogue with India

**Claim.** In January 2023, Sharif called for “serious and sincere talks” with Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi over Kashmir and other disputes. He said Pakistan had learned from three wars and wanted peace, while warning that another war between nuclear-armed neighbors would bring catastrophic consequences.

**Why it matters.** This is direct evidence that Sharif publicly endorsed a peaceful alternative to escalation.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan has learnt its lesson: Shehbaz Sharif calls for honest talks with PM Modi,” *Times of India*, 17 January 2023, [direct link](https://timesofindia.indiatimes.com/world/pakistan/pakistan-has-learnt-its-lesson-shehbaz-sharif-calls-for-honest-talks-with-pm-modi/articleshow/97048436.cms), paragraphs 76–110.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. The statement was made by Sharif in his capacity as prime minister.

**Complication.** Sharif conditioned talks on India reversing its 5 August 2019 Kashmir decision. India could therefore regard the proposal as politically conditional rather than an unconditional peace initiative.

**Cautions.** The report is based on an interview through a Dubai-based Arabic channel and includes Pakistani and Indian framing. The statement was rhetoric, not a bilateral agreement.

**Independent corroboration.** [Indian Express analysis of Sharif’s peace statement](https://indianexpress.com/article/explained/explained-global/shehbaz-peace-with-india-pak-pms-statement-8387904); [APP report on Kashmir diplomacy](https://www.app.com.pk/national/sustainable-peace-stability-in-sa-depends-on-resolution-of-kashmir-dispute-pm).

### WEB-2B-002 — The Line of Control ceasefire largely held

**Claim.** A UN official reported that the India–Pakistan ceasefire along the Line of Control remained in place in 2023, with only two violations reported during the year. UNMOGIP continued monitoring the LoC, although visa and access restrictions hindered its work.

**Why it matters.** Continued observance of the ceasefire is the clearest measurable regional-peace outcome during Sharif’s period.

**Strongest source.** “Ceasefire along LoC in disputed Kashmir is holding: UN official,” Associated Press of Pakistan, 24 February 2024, reporting the UN briefing on 2023, [direct link](https://www.app.com.pk/global/ceasefire-along-loc-in-disputed-kashmir-is-holding-un-official/), paragraphs 160–170.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, reported retrospectively in 2024.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The ceasefire was an inherited bilateral arrangement and depended heavily on military command, not only Sharif’s cabinet.

**Complication.** The same source reports UNMOGIP access problems and continued disagreement over Kashmir. A quiet ceasefire did not resolve the underlying dispute.

**Cautions.** The source is Pakistan’s official news agency reporting a UN briefing and may reproduce Pakistan’s framing about which side obstructed UN access. The “two violations” figure should be checked against UNMOGIP records.

**Independent corroboration.** [UNMOGIP mandate and monitoring role](https://unmogip.unmissions.org/en/about-unmogip); [Indian government ceasefire reporting](https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleseDetailm.aspx?PRID=1947695).

### WEB-2B-003 — Sharif pursued regional cooperation through the SCO

**Claim.** In his 4 July 2023 SCO address, Sharif called for regional cooperation, opposed using terrorism for diplomatic point-scoring, urged collective action through SCO-RATS, and called for engagement with Afghanistan alongside demands that Afghan authorities prevent their territory from being used for terrorism.

**Why it matters.** This shows a diplomatic alternative to unilateral escalation: multilateral pressure, regional cooperation, and conditional engagement.

**Strongest source.** “Statement by H.E. Mr. Muhammad Shahbaz Sharif during the SCO Council of Heads of State Meeting,” Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Pakistan, 4 July 2023, [direct link](https://mofa.gov.pk/statement-by-h-e-mr-muhammad-shahbaz-sharif-prime-minister-of-pakistan-during-the-sco-council-of-heads-of-state-meeting-4th-july-2023-virtual-format), paragraphs 108–131.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. This was Sharif’s official speech as prime minister.

**Complication.** The speech also demanded concrete action from Afghanistan and described terrorism as a threat requiring forceful collective response. Diplomatic language did not eliminate coercive pressure.

**Cautions.** It is an official foreign-policy statement and therefore strong evidence of rhetoric and formal objectives, but not of implementation or outcome.

**Independent corroboration.** [UN Security Council record on Afghanistan](https://digitallibrary.un.org/nanna/record/4033247/files/S_PV.9515-EN.pdf?registerDownload=1&version=1&withMetadata=0&withWatermark=0); [UNHCR Pakistan–Afghanistan dashboard](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/107878).

### WEB-2B-004 — Militant violence increased and civilians remained exposed

**Claim.** Human Rights Watch reported that TTP, al-Qaeda, BLA, ISKP, and affiliates carried out suicide bombings and other indiscriminate attacks in 2023, causing hundreds of civilian deaths and injuries. The U.S. State Department also reported a significant increase in attacks on police and security forces, while CRSS recorded a major year-on-year increase in violence-related fatalities.

**Why it matters.** Pakistan faced genuine and severe security threats, which complicates assessment of the government’s use of force and its failure to prevent attacks.

**Strongest source.** *World Report 2024: Pakistan*, Human Rights Watch, 2024, [direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan), paragraphs 93–96 and 147–158.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif’s government directed federal counterterrorism policy, but violence was concentrated in provinces and areas where military and provincial institutions exercised major autonomy.

**Complication.** The attacks were conducted by non-state armed groups and included major attacks on civilians, political gatherings, police, and military personnel. The government had legitimate security responsibilities.

**Cautions.** HRW and the U.S. State Department use different datasets and rely partly on reported incidents. Attribution of attacks and exact casualty totals can be contested.

**Independent corroboration.** [CRSS 2023 security report](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/); [UN Security Council condemnation of the Peshawar attack](https://press.un.org/en/2023/sc15188.doc.htm); [U.S. State Department 2023 report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html).

### WEB-2B-005 — Pakistan demanded Afghan action against militant sanctuaries

**Claim.** After the July 2023 Bajaur bombing, Sharif urged Afghanistan’s interim government to prevent the use of Afghan territory by militants and alleged that Afghan citizens and cross-border sanctuaries were involved in attacks against Pakistan.

**Why it matters.** This was a central security justification for diplomatic pressure on Kabul and later coercive refugee policy.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan urges Kabul for action against use of Afghan soil by militants,” *Dawn*/APP, 1 August 2023, [direct link](https://www.dawn.com/news/1767872/pakistan-urges-kabul-for-action-against-use-of-afghan-soil-by-militants).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. The statement was made by Sharif during an official visit after the attack.

**Complication.** Afghanistan’s Taliban authorities denied or disputed Pakistani allegations concerning militant sanctuary. The evidence does not establish that the Afghan government directed the attacks.

**Cautions.** The report reproduces Pakistani government assertions. Independent access to militant bases and border areas was limited.

**Independent corroboration.** [Pakistan’s SCO address](https://mofa.gov.pk/statement-by-h-e-mr-muhammad-shahbaz-sharif-prime-minister-of-pakistan-during-the-sco-council-of-heads-of-state-meeting-4th-july-2023-virtual-format), paragraphs 115–121; [U.S. State Department human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html); [CRSS security report](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/).

### WEB-2B-006 — Afghanistan claims were difficult to independently verify

**Claim.** Pakistan’s claims that Afghan soil was being used by TTP and related groups were made amid rising attacks, but public evidence was incomplete and access to affected areas was restricted. The U.S. State Department reported that violence in Balochistan and former FATA areas was often not reported, while intimidation by both militants and security forces limited human-rights and journalistic access.

**Why it matters.** The credibility of force decisions and coercive diplomacy depended on evidence that was difficult for legislators, courts, journalists, and outside observers to test.

**Strongest source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Pakistan*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, [direct link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 141–146.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The federal government controlled public claims and diplomatic demands, but information restrictions reflected military, provincial, and security-agency authority as well.

**Complication.** Restricted access also resulted from genuine danger posed by militant groups and difficult terrain, not only state censorship.

**Cautions.** The U.S. State Department report compiles information from government, NGOs, media, and other sources; some allegations cannot be independently resolved.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRW 2024 Pakistan report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan); [CRSS annual security report](https://crss.pk/annual-security-report-2023-pakistan/).

### WEB-2B-007 — Afghan deportation policy created protection risks

**Claim.** In October 2023, Pakistan announced a 28-day deadline for undocumented migrants to leave, threatening forced deportation. HRW reported that Pakistani authorities identified approximately 1.73 million unregistered Afghans and justified the policy by claiming Afghan nationals were involved in most suicide bombings. UNHCR and other UN bodies warned that returns should be voluntary and free of pressure.

**Why it matters.** This was a major international-security and humanitarian measure, but it occurred after Sharif left office and should primarily be attributed to the caretaker government and security establishment.

**Strongest source.** *World Report 2024: Pakistan*, Human Rights Watch, 2024, [direct link](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan), paragraphs 103–106.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns late 2023; mainly caretaker-period evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak for Sharif personally. His government created the security and political context, but the formal October decision was made after his premiership.

**Complication.** Pakistan faced real cross-border militant threats and argued that the policy applied to undocumented foreigners generally, not Afghans alone.

**Cautions.** Population estimates and return figures varied. The security claim linking Afghan nationals to most suicide bombings was not independently established in the source.

**Independent corroboration.** [UNHCR Pakistan country information](https://www.unhcr.org/where-we-work/countries/pakistan); [Associated Press report on UN warnings](https://apnews.com/article/pakistan-migrant-crackdown-afghans-human-rights-1a99ea9d909620661d419709831cdced); [UNHCR emergency update](https://www.unhcr.org/sites/default/files/2024-01/UNHCR_Pakistan-Afghanistan_Returns_Emergency_Response_Update_8.pdf).

### WEB-2B-008 — Counterterrorism operations involved alleged unlawful harm and weak remedy

**Claim.** The U.S. State Department reported credible allegations of extrajudicial killings, enforced disappearances, torture, and weak investigation and prosecution of security-force abuses in 2023. It also reported that security forces conducted counterinsurgency operations in Balochistan and other areas, often without transparent public reporting.

**Why it matters.** A government may face legitimate armed threats while still violating lawful-security obligations through unaccountable operations.

**Strongest source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Pakistan*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, [direct link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 8–17, 21–31, and 141–155.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with some inherited patterns.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif was head of the federal government and formally responsible for national security, but individual operations were conducted by military, paramilitary, intelligence, and provincial bodies.

**Complication.** Militants carried out attacks against civilians and security forces, and authorities did conduct arrests, weapons seizures, and counterterrorism operations.

**Cautions.** Many allegations were difficult to verify because victims, journalists, and investigators faced intimidation and restricted access. The report distinguishes credible reports from definitive legal findings.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf); [Human Rights Watch](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan).

### WEB-2B-009 — Military resources addressed real threats but lacked transparency

**Claim.** SIPRI’s 2023 military-expenditure data recorded approximately $9.78 billion in Pakistani military spending. The security environment included major militant attacks and counterterrorism operations, but the inspected public record did not establish whether procurement and military spending were proportionate, transparently audited, or insulated from military-political patronage.

**Why it matters.** Security spending may have been necessary, but this lens requires distinguishing genuine defense needs from opaque institutional power and economic privilege.

**Strongest source.** SIPRI military expenditure data as supplied in the local briefing, corroborated by the broader 2023 security record; public narrative source: [CRSS annual security report](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/).

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for federal budget authorization; weak for detailed procurement and military expenditure decisions.

**Complication.** Pakistan faced a genuine surge in attacks, including attacks on police, soldiers, political gatherings, and civilians.

**Cautions.** Public expenditure totals do not show procurement quality, corruption, or operational proportionality. The local SIPRI figure is not a ruler-attribution claim.

**Independent corroboration.** [SIPRI military expenditure database](https://milex.sipri.org/sipri); [World Bank Pakistan overview](https://www.worldbank.org/ext/en/country/pakistan).

### WEB-2B-010 — Domestic political violence weakened lawful security oversight

**Claim.** After the 9 May 2023 unrest, thousands of PTI supporters were arrested and some civilians were referred to military courts. The U.S. State Department reported that the National Assembly demanded military trials, while the Supreme Court later declared such trials unconstitutional before subsequent litigation continued.

**Why it matters.** Although primarily a domestic political-freedom issue, the episode affected lawful security governance, judicial oversight, and the credibility of official force claims.

**Strongest source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Pakistan*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, [direct link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 111–116 and 297–300.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the federal coalition and cabinet; responsibility was shared with the military, prosecution authorities, courts, and provincial governments.

**Complication.** Protesters attacked military installations, police, ambulances, schools, and public property. Authorities had a legitimate duty to investigate and prosecute violence.

**Cautions.** This evidence concerns domestic repression more directly than international peace and should not be over-weighted in chapter 2B.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRW 2024 Pakistan report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan); [Amnesty International statement](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/pakistan-civilians-must-not-be-tried-under-military-laws); [HRCP annual report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf).

### WEB-2B-011 — Peace rhetoric coexisted with maximalist Kashmir conditions

**Claim.** Sharif’s public diplomacy supported peaceful coexistence and negotiation, but Pakistan continued to insist that India reverse its August 2019 Kashmir measures before talks could proceed. Pakistan’s official position also continued to frame Kashmir through self-determination and UN Security Council resolutions.

**Why it matters.** The record shows restraint in rhetoric and posture, but not a substantive settlement strategy capable of resolving the dispute.

**Strongest source.** Sharif’s January interview as reported by *Times of India*, 17 January 2023, [direct link](https://timesofindia.indiatimes.com/world/pakistan/pakistan-has-learnt-its-lesson-shehbaz-sharif-calls-for-honest-talks-with-pm-modi/articleshow/97048436.cms), paragraphs 92–110.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with inherited dispute conditions.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for rhetoric; moderate for underlying policy, which was shared with the military and foreign-policy establishment.

**Complication.** Conditioning talks on India’s reversal of a constitutional decision may have made negotiations politically difficult from India’s perspective.

**Cautions.** This is a media account of an interview and official Pakistani position; Indian and Kashmiri perspectives require separate examination.

**Independent corroboration.** [Pakistan Foreign Ministry SCO speech](https://mofa.gov.pk/statement-by-h-e-mr-muhammad-shahbaz-sharif-prime-minister-of-pakistan-during-the-sco-council-of-heads-of-state-meeting-4th-july-2023-virtual-format), paragraphs 127–131; [UNMOGIP](https://unmogip.unmissions.org/en/about-unmogip).

### WEB-2B-012 — No durable regional settlement was achieved

**Claim.** Pakistan ended 2023 without a new India–Pakistan settlement, an Afghanistan security agreement, or a durable mechanism resolving TTP sanctuary allegations and border tensions. The inherited LoC ceasefire remained an important stabilizing arrangement, but underlying disputes persisted.

**Why it matters.** The period produced limited restraint but no demonstrably safer or more lawful long-term regional-security order.

**Strongest source.** Combined review of [UNMOGIP reporting](https://www.app.com.pk/global/ceasefire-along-loc-in-disputed-kashmir-is-holding-un-official/), [Sharif’s SCO address](https://mofa.gov.pk/statement-by-h-e-mr-muhammad-shahbaz-sharif-prime-minister-of-pakistan-during-the-sco-council-of-heads-of-state-meeting-4th-july-2023-virtual-format), and [HRW’s 2024 Pakistan report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan).

**Period/status.** End-of-period assessment based on 2023 evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif had limited time and authority; after August, the caretaker government governed.

**Complication.** The absence of a new settlement does not mean the government failed to preserve peace. The LoC ceasefire largely held, and open war was avoided.

**Cautions.** This is a negative finding from inspected public records, not proof that no confidential negotiations occurred.

**Independent corroboration.** [UNMOGIP mandate](https://unmogip.unmissions.org/en/about-unmogip); [UNHCR Pakistan–Afghanistan dashboard](https://data.unhcr.org/en/documents/details/107878).

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **2B.1 — Peaceful alternatives:** **Partially evidenced.** Sharif publicly pursued dialogue with India and multilateral engagement on Afghanistan. However, talks were conditional and no durable settlement resulted.
- **2B.2 — Starting or prolonging war:** **No evidence that Sharif initiated or unnecessarily prolonged an interstate war of choice in 2023.** Pakistan faced serious militant violence, but cross-border coercion against Afghanistan and the Afghan deportation policy require separate attribution analysis.
- **2B.3 — Honest justification for force:** **Mixed and unresolved.** Pakistan had genuine security threats, but claims about Afghan sanctuary and Afghan nationals’ role in attacks were difficult to independently verify. Public justification was not always accompanied by reviewable evidence.
- **2B.4 — Civilian and prisoner protection:** **Adverse evidence.** Reports documented alleged extrajudicial killings, disappearances, torture, arbitrary detention, and protection risks to Afghan refugees. The evidence is strongest for failures of lawful remedy, but individual command attribution remains incomplete.
- **2B.5 — Control of proxies and arms:** **Insufficient evidence.** Pakistan faced attacks by TTP, BLA, and ISKP, but the inspected record does not establish whether Pakistan supported, tolerated, or effectively controlled foreign proxies during 2023.
- **2B.6 — Scrutiny of war claims:** **Adverse/mixed.** Access restrictions, intimidation, censorship, and limited reporting from Balochistan and former FATA impeded independent scrutiny. Courts and international reporting provided some checks.
- **2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements:** **Partially evidenced.** The LoC ceasefire largely held, and Sharif advocated dialogue. No new settlement or Afghanistan agreement was achieved.
- **2B.8 — Responsible military resources:** **Unresolved.** Spending addressed real security threats, but the public record does not establish transparency, proportionality, procurement integrity, or civilian oversight.
- **2B.9 — Accountability and remedy:** **Adverse evidence.** The State Department and HRW reported weak investigation and prosecution of security-force abuses, enforced disappearances, and impunity. The government did not demonstrate comprehensive remedy.
- **2B.10 — Lasting international peace:** **Unresolved/partial.** Pakistan avoided war with India and maintained the LoC ceasefire, but Afghanistan relations deteriorated and no durable regional settlement or accountability mechanism was created.

## 5. Information environment

Research was difficult because:

- Military and intelligence institutions had major influence over Afghanistan, Kashmir, counterterrorism, and border policy.
- Access to Balochistan, former FATA, and conflict areas was restricted by both militants and state authorities.
- Official claims about militant sanctuaries, Afghan involvement, and counterterrorism casualties were difficult to independently verify.
- Victims, journalists, missing-person families, and human-rights organizations faced intimidation and harassment.
- Security data varied across CRSS, PICSS, the South Asia Terrorism Portal, official agencies, and international reporting.
- India–Pakistan reporting was strongly polarized, with each side presenting competing accounts of ceasefire violations and Kashmir.
- Refugee numbers and returns varied by government, UNHCR, IOM, and media source.
- The late-2023 Afghan deportation campaign occurred during caretaker rule, complicating attribution to Sharif.

## 7. Counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate URLs inspected or attempted: 16.
- Final evidence records: 12.
- Main independent source families retained: UN/UNMOGIP, Pakistani official records, Human Rights Watch, U.S. Department of State, CRSS, UNHCR, and reputable reporting.
- Main unresolved questions: Pakistan’s precise support or tolerance of proxies; command responsibility for cross-border or counterterrorism abuses; military procurement and budget transparency; the exact legal basis and cabinet responsibility for Afghan deportations; and whether confidential India or Afghanistan negotiations occurred.
- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection: Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister until 9 August 2023. He formally headed the federal government, cabinet, National Security Committee, and national security administration. In practice, the military and intelligence services retained major influence over counterterrorism, internal security, political detention, media controls, and Balochistan. Provincial governments controlled much routine policing, so provincial incidents cannot automatically be attributed personally to Sharif.

Pakistan entered 2023 with inherited militant violence, enforced disappearances, police abuse, political polarization, and weak accountability. During Sharif’s tenure, the most consequential domestic-security event was the May 2023 crackdown following Imran Khan’s arrest. PTI supporters committed serious violence, including attacks on military sites and public property; the state responded with mass arrests, military detention, internet shutdowns, disappearances, and military-court proceedings.

The evidence indicates that Pakistan faced genuine non-state security threats, but the federal coalition and security institutions also used exceptional powers broadly against political opponents and dissenters. The strongest adverse evidence concerns arbitrary detention, enforced disappearance, torture allegations, military trials of civilians, restrictions on peaceful assembly, and lack of effective accountability. Late-2023 caretaker actions, including the Afghan deportation campaign, require separate attribution.
