## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-3B-001 — Continuing enforced disappearance, killing, and torture allegations

**Claim.** HRCP reported that enforced disappearances, extrajudicial killings, and torture by state agencies continued in 2023 without signs of abatement. It also cited official Sindh police data recording 3,296 police encounters in one year, while acknowledging that the full picture could be larger or incomplete.

**Why it matters.** This is core evidence concerning protection from state abuse and coercive-force control.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, Human Rights Commission of Pakistan, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 5–6, lines 345–355.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with some inherited patterns.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The federal government was formally responsible for national security and oversight, but operations were distributed across military, intelligence, police, and provincial agencies.

**Complication.** Pakistan faced serious militant and insurgent threats, and some police encounters may have involved genuine armed suspects.

**Cautions.** HRCP states that official reports, press accounts, and NGO surveys may not show the complete picture. The record contains allegations and trends, not a comprehensive judicial finding against Sharif personally.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. State Department 2023 report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 8–17 and 141–155; [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2024](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan).

### WEB-3B-002 — Mass arrests after the 9 May unrest

**Claim.** After Imran Khan’s arrest on 9 May, PTI supporters attacked military and public installations. The state responded with mass arrests, including of political opponents and people who had not been shown to have committed violence. HRW reported more than 4,000 arrests by 20 May and warned that vague public-order laws were being used against opposition members.

**Why it matters.** The government had a legitimate duty to prosecute violence, but the evidence indicates that detention extended beyond clearly documented perpetrators.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan: Mass Arrests Target Political Opposition,” Human Rights Watch, 20 May 2023, [direct link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/05/20/pakistan-mass-arrests-target-political-opposition), paragraphs 573–590.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong. The federal coalition, interior authorities, police, military, and provincial governments jointly directed the response.

**Complication.** The unrest involved arson, attacks on police, military installations, ambulances, schools, and public property. Some arrests were therefore legally justified.

**Cautions.** HRW is an advocacy organization and its release relies partly on local reporting and opposition accounts. The distinction between lawful arrests and arbitrary detention requires case-level review.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6; [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 111–116.

### WEB-3B-003 — Civilians tried in military courts

**Claim.** In May and June 2023, authorities announced and legislatively supported military trials for civilians accused of attacking military installations during the 9 May unrest. The Supreme Court declared the trials unconstitutional in October, but another bench suspended that order in December and allowed proceedings to continue.

**Why it matters.** The use of military courts for civilians is central to lawful detention, judicial oversight, and restrained crisis response.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 5–6, lines 283–344.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the federal coalition and cabinet; responsibility was shared with military authorities, Parliament, prosecutors, and the courts.

**Complication.** The underlying attacks on military installations were serious and required investigation and prosecution.

**Cautions.** The legal status changed during the year. The record should be paired with the Supreme Court’s judgments and individual case files.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 113–116; [Amnesty International candidate statement](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/pakistan-civilians-must-not-be-tried-under-military-laws).

### WEB-3B-004 — Expansion of intelligence and security powers

**Claim.** Parliament passed amendments in July 2023 to the Official Secrets Act and Pakistan Army Act. HRCP reported that the Official Secrets amendments expanded intelligence agencies’ authority to enter and search persons or places without warrants and broadened the ability to target dissidents and political rivals. The Army Act amendment criminalized defamation of the armed forces, including online.

**Why it matters.** These amendments expanded exceptional coercive powers during a politically charged period and increased risks of arbitrary surveillance, detention, and intimidation.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), p. 6, lines 331–337.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the governing coalition and Parliament; practical implementation depended on intelligence agencies and the military.

**Complication.** The government could justify stronger intelligence powers by citing terrorism, espionage, and the 9 May attacks.

**Cautions.** HRCP’s account is critical and should be supplemented with the gazetted statutory text and parliamentary debate. The extent of actual use during 2023 remains incompletely documented.

**Independent corroboration.** [Freedom House, Freedom in the World 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023), paragraphs 102–118; [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 136–140.

### WEB-3B-005 — Arrests and violence against peaceful Baloch and women-led protests

**Claim.** HRCP reported that the Baloch women’s march led by Mahrang Baloch faced arrests and police violence in Dera Ghazi Khan and Islamabad. It also reported that Section 144 was used extensively to pre-empt assemblies and that courts, rather than police or administrators, were often responsible for allowing peaceful marches to proceed.

**Why it matters.** This is direct evidence about physical safety during protest and whether policing protected or retaliated against dissent.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 6–7, lines 366–374.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The events involved Islamabad police, provincial authorities, deputy commissioners, and security institutions. National attribution is limited unless federal direction or tolerance is shown.

**Complication.** Authorities may have cited public-order and security concerns, particularly during a period of militant threats and political unrest.

**Cautions.** HRCP’s report aggregates incidents across jurisdictions and does not establish a single federal command order.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP statement on Baloch protesters](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/government-must-hear-out-baloch-protestors-release-jailed-women); [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 8–10 and 141–155.

### WEB-3B-006 — Internet shutdowns and journalist disappearance

**Claim.** Freedom House reported that the government imposed a four-day internet shutdown in May 2023 and blocked social-media platforms amid the confrontation with Imran Khan. It also reported that journalist Imran Riaz Khan disappeared in May during the crackdown and returned in September without publicly explaining his disappearance.

**Why it matters.** Internet shutdowns and disappearances directly impaired public safety reporting, accountability, and the ability of victims and witnesses to communicate.

**Strongest source.** *Freedom on the Net 2023: Pakistan*, Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-net/2023), paragraphs 30–40.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for federal telecommunications and security policy; the disappearance itself cannot be attributed to Sharif without a documented chain of command.

**Complication.** The government could claim the shutdowns were temporary measures to prevent incitement and protect life during violent unrest.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is an advocacy-based comparative organization. The identity of those responsible for the journalist’s disappearance remained unresolved.

**Independent corroboration.** [CPJ candidate report](https://cpj.org/2023/05/pakistan-crackdown-on-media); [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 6–7.

### WEB-3B-007 — Serious non-state militant violence

**Claim.** HRW reported that TTP, al-Qaeda, BLA, ISKP, and affiliates conducted suicide bombings and other attacks in 2023, causing hundreds of civilian deaths and injuries. The U.S. State Department reported at least 386 deaths of police and military personnel in terrorist attacks and counterterrorism operations during the first three quarters.

**Why it matters.** Domestic safety must account for both state abuse and genuine non-state violence. The government inherited a severe security problem and had legitimate reasons to conduct counterterrorism operations.

**Strongest source.** [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2024: Pakistan](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan), paragraphs 93–96 and 147–158.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif’s federal government directed national security policy, but violence and policing were substantially provincial and military-led.

**Complication.** The attacks were conducted by armed non-state groups and included attacks on civilians, police, political gatherings, religious sites, and military personnel.

**Cautions.** Different datasets produce different totals. Reported group responsibility is sometimes based on claims rather than independent forensic findings.

**Independent corroboration.** [CRSS 2023 security report](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/); [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 10–20.

### WEB-3B-008 — Mob violence and attacks on minorities

**Claim.** HRCP recorded approximately 34 victims in at least 26 mob-lynching incidents in 2023, with 16 incidents in Karachi. It also described the Jaranwala assault in which Christian churches and homes were attacked and burned.

**Why it matters.** These events show failures of public protection from communal and mob violence, while also requiring distinction between national policy and local policing failures.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 2, 6–7, lines 159–164 and 345–355.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. The attacks were local and provincial, but the federal government had obligations regarding minority protection, policing standards, and accountability.

**Complication.** Some mob incidents concerned alleged theft or criminality rather than religion. The state did not itself organize the mob violence according to the evidence reviewed.

**Cautions.** HRCP states that its media-monitoring count may understate the actual total. Individual cases require local police and court records.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraph 8; [HRW Pakistan report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan).

### WEB-3B-009 — Vulnerable groups remained inadequately protected

**Claim.** HRCP reported continuing violence and discrimination affecting women, children, religious minorities, transgender people, refugees, and other vulnerable groups. It recorded more than 4,000 reported child-sexual-abuse cases, at least 26 million out-of-school children, violence against women, and ongoing risks to Afghan migrants and refugees.

**Why it matters.** This establishes a broad protection gap, though ordinary education and poverty outcomes should remain in 6B unless directly connected to physical insecurity.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 6–8, lines 376–406.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for federal law and national protection policy; lower for provincial implementation and social violence.

**Complication.** Some abuse was committed by families, employers, mobs, or other non-state actors. The federal government did enact or retain protective laws and programs, though implementation was weak.

**Cautions.** HRCP uses official, media, NGO, and correspondent data and warns that figures may not be complete. “Failure to protect” should not be converted automatically into direct personal responsibility.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html); [Human Rights Watch report](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan).

### WEB-3B-010 — Weak and selective accountability

**Claim.** The U.S. State Department reported that the government rarely took credible steps to identify and punish officials implicated in human-rights abuses. It also reported lengthy delays, weak investigations, selective accountability, and limited access for NGOs to political detainees.

**Why it matters.** The absence of effective remedy is central to whether a ruler controlled coercive forces or tolerated impunity.

**Strongest source.** *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Pakistan*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, [direct link](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 8–16, 21–31, and 120–124.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023, with longer-running institutional patterns.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The federal government could reform prosecution, oversight, and police accountability, but the judiciary, military, provincial governments, and intelligence bodies were partially autonomous.

**Complication.** Some courts did intervene, including the October Supreme Court ruling against military trials of civilians, and official commissions continued to process disappearance cases.

**Cautions.** The report combines official and NGO information and does not establish culpability for individual officials in every case.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP annual report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf); [Freedom House 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023).

### WEB-3B-011 — Military primacy constrained Sharif’s control

**Claim.** Freedom House reported that the military retained primacy over national security, relations with Afghanistan, India, China, and the United States, and domestic counterterrorism. It also reported that military intelligence agencies operated with little oversight and were accused of abducting, detaining, interrogating, and torturing individuals.

**Why it matters.** This is essential for calibrating attribution. Sharif was formally responsible but operated within a security system where military autonomy sharply limited civilian control.

**Strongest source.** *Freedom in the World 2023: Pakistan*, Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023), paragraphs 102–118.

**Period/status.** Inherited institutional condition applicable during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for formal responsibility; limited for direct operational control.

**Complication.** Civilian authorities retained formal policy and budget powers and sometimes cooperated with or enabled military institutions.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is an advocacy-oriented comparative source and describes allegations as part of a broader political assessment.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 5–6; [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html).

### WEB-3B-012 — No demonstrated durable improvement in safety

**Claim.** By the end of 2023, Pakistan had not demonstrably become safer from either state or non-state violence. Militant violence increased, allegations of state abuse continued, political detention and disappearance remained unresolved, and civilian protection remained uneven across regions.

**Why it matters.** The evidence supports an end-state characterized by persistent or worsening exposure rather than a clearly safer institutional order.

**Strongest source.** Cross-source synthesis of [HRCP](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), [HRW](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/pakistan), and the [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html).

**Period/status.** End-of-period assessment.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate but not exclusive. Sharif governed only part of the year and inherited powerful military, provincial, judicial, and militant constraints.

**Complication.** The government did respond to militant attacks, courts provided some remedies, and the May unrest was not solely state-created.

**Cautions.** No single dataset measures “domestic safety” comprehensively. This is a calibrated synthesis, not a score.

**Independent corroboration.** [CRSS 2023 report](https://crss.pk/pakistans-violence-related-fatalities-mark-a-record-6-year-high-56-surge-in-violence-recorded-in-2023/); [Freedom House Freedom on the Net](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-net/2023).

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **3B.1 — Protection from state abuse:** **Adverse evidence.** Enforced disappearances, torture allegations, arbitrary detention, and extrajudicial-killing allegations continued. The federal government did not demonstrate comprehensive remedy.
- **3B.2 — Control of coercive forces:** **Adverse/mixed.** The state confronted serious militancy, but military and intelligence agencies operated with limited civilian oversight and were linked to repeated abuse allegations.
- **3B.3 — Incitement and targeted hatred:** **Insufficient direct evidence of Sharif personally inciting violence.** However, official scapegoating of PTI opponents and Afghan migrants, and weak protection against communal violence, require further research.
- **3B.4 — Complaints and independent oversight:** **Adverse/mixed.** Courts sometimes intervened, but disappearance commissions, police investigations, and accountability mechanisms were slow, selective, or ineffective.
- **3B.5 — Emergency and surveillance powers:** **Adverse evidence.** July legislation expanded intelligence powers; internet shutdowns, Section 144, surveillance, and military courts were used broadly during political crises.
- **3B.6 — Safety without state terror:** **Unresolved but adverse overall.** Militant violence posed a real threat, yet counterterrorism and political-security responses generated a broader climate of fear.
- **3B.7 — Protection of vulnerable groups:** **Adverse evidence.** Women, children, minorities, transgender people, Afghans, and Baloch communities remained exposed to violence and weak remedies.
- **3B.8 — Safe protest and dissent:** **Adverse evidence.** Peaceful Baloch and women-led protests faced arrest and police violence; May violence also led to excessive and indiscriminate detention.
- **3B.9 — Restrained crisis response:** **Adverse evidence.** The government responded to May unrest with mass arrests, military trials, shutdowns, and expanded coercive powers. The violence committed by protesters was serious, but the response was not consistently narrow or proportionate.
- **3B.10 — Lasting domestic safety:** **Unresolved/adverse.** No durable improvement was demonstrated by year-end; both militant violence and allegations of state abuse remained significant.

## 5. Information environment

Research was difficult because:

- Military and intelligence agencies operated with limited public oversight.
- Victims of disappearance, detainees, journalists, opposition activists, and Baloch organizers faced intimidation.
- Access to Balochistan, former FATA, prisons, and security operations was restricted.
- Government data on detainees, police encounters, disappearances, and military-court cases were incomplete or contested.
- HRCP specifically warned that exact figures were unavailable for many detainee and disappearance categories.
- Political polarization produced sharply conflicting accounts of the May unrest and the state response.
- Courts provided some independent scrutiny, but their decisions were contested and sometimes suspended.
- The caretaker government governed after August, complicating attribution for late-2023 conduct.

### C. Inspected but not retained as final evidence

- None of the accessible sources was rejected outright. Amnesty, CPJ, and NCHR pages were not sufficiently retrievable for detailed extraction and are therefore treated as corroborating or blocked rather than final evidence.
- Official government claims that arrests and restrictions were necessary for public order are retained as counterevidence, not independently verified findings.

## 7. Counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate URLs inspected or attempted: 16.
- Final evidence records: 12.

- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Political Freedom vs. Authoritarian Rule: Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister from 11 April 2022 until the National Assembly was dissolved on 9 August 2023. His Pakistan Democratic Movement coalition governed through Parliament during the first part of 2023; a caretaker federal government then governed for the rest of the year.

Pakistan retained competitive multiparty institutions, an active Parliament, courts, civil society, and a diverse media sector. However, political freedom was heavily constrained by the military establishment, which retained major influence over national security, counterterrorism, media boundaries, and the treatment of the opposition. Sharif’s government generally avoided confrontation with the military and cooperated with it during the crackdown on Imran Khan’s PTI.

The defining political event was the 9 May unrest following Khan’s arrest. PTI supporters attacked military installations and public property. The state response included mass arrests, disappearances, internet shutdowns, media restrictions, pressure on PTI politicians to abandon the party, and military trials of civilians. Some restrictions followed genuine violence, but independent monitors found that enforcement extended to peaceful opposition and weakened the conditions for free political competition.

The year also saw postponed provincial elections, a disputed caretaker period, rushed legislation affecting media and intelligence powers, and continuing restrictions on journalists and digital speech. Courts sometimes constrained the executive and security apparatus, but their interventions were inconsistent and occasionally suspended.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main lenses |
|---|---|---|
| WEB-4B-001 | Political competition remained formally multiparty but the military constrained civilian politics | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.5 |
| WEB-4B-002 | Provincial elections were delayed and caretaker governments exceeded ordinary limits | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-003 | The May 2023 unrest was followed by mass arrests of PTI supporters and opponents | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-004 | Authorities used military courts and political pressure against PTI | 4B.2, 4B.3, 4B.4 |
| WEB-4B-005 | Rushed July/August legislation expanded censorship and intelligence powers | 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-006 | Independent press freedom was constrained by censorship, intimidation, and legal pressure | 4B.3, 4B.6 |
| WEB-4B-007 | Digital controls and internet shutdowns restricted political communication | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.9 |
| WEB-4B-008 | Courts sometimes constrained executive and caretaker overreach | 4B.4, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-009 | Political rights were unequal for women, minorities, Ahmadis, and marginalized ethnic groups | 4B.7 |
| WEB-4B-010 | There was no demonstrated strengthening of democratic resilience by year-end | 4B.10 |

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-4B-001 — Formal democracy constrained by military influence

**Claim.** Freedom House reported that Pakistan formally had elected civilian institutions, but the military retained primacy over national security, relations with Afghanistan, India, China, and the United States, and domestic counterterrorism. It described the military as more powerful than the judiciary or elected government in setting the limits of civilian politics.

**Why it matters.** Sharif’s formal authority cannot be assessed without recognizing the military’s practical veto power over core political and security issues.

**Strongest source.** *Freedom in the World 2023: Pakistan*, Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023), paragraphs 87 and 101–118.

**Period/status.** Inherited institutional condition applicable during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif did not create the civil-military imbalance, but his government generally sought accommodation with the army and did not materially reduce its political role.

**Complication.** Civilian institutions remained active: Parliament legislated, parties competed, and courts sometimes checked government decisions.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is a comparative advocacy-oriented source and synthesizes a broad political pattern rather than proving each individual decision.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 5–6; [RSF Pakistan profile](https://rsf.org/en/country/pakistan).

### WEB-4B-002 — Delayed elections and caretaker overreach

**Claim.** Provincial assemblies in Punjab and Khyber Pakhtunkhwa were dissolved early, creating a constitutional expectation of provincial elections. HRCP reported that the elections were repeatedly postponed and that caretaker governments remained in office beyond the expected period. It also reported a Lahore High Court finding that Punjab’s caretaker government exceeded its powers by making major policy decisions.

**Why it matters.** Election timing and caretaker neutrality directly affect whether power is genuinely contestable and whether constitutional succession rules are respected.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6, lines 259–318.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Provincial assembly dissolutions were initiated by PTI-led provincial governments, but the federal government, Election Commission, courts, and caretaker administrations all affected the outcome.

**Complication.** Authorities cited security, funding, and administrative constraints. The federal government did not alone control the Election Commission or provincial governments.

**Cautions.** The cited HRCP report is critical of the postponements; court records and Election Commission documents should be added for a complete legal chronology.

**Independent corroboration.** [UK House of Commons Library briefing](https://commonslibrary.parliament.uk/research-briefings/cbp-9883); [President of Pakistan statement on caretaker legality](https://president.gov.pk/news/president-concerned-about-legality-of-caretaker-govts-beyond-90-days-2); [FAFEN publications](https://fafen.org/publications).

### WEB-4B-003 — Mass arrests after the May unrest

**Claim.** After Imran Khan’s arrest on 9 May, PTI supporters attacked military installations and public property. The government and security agencies responded with mass arrests. HRW reported that more than 4,000 people were detained by 20 May, including opposition members and people not shown to have engaged in violence.

**Why it matters.** The government had to respond to serious violence, but arbitrary or politically selective arrests undermine genuine electoral competition and opposition rights.

**Strongest source.** “Pakistan: Mass Arrests Target Political Opposition,” Human Rights Watch, 20 May 2023, [direct link](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/05/20/pakistan-mass-arrests-target-political-opposition), paragraphs 573–590.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong. The federal coalition, Interior Ministry, police, military, and provincial authorities jointly shaped the response.

**Complication.** Protesters attacked the Lahore corps commander’s residence, military facilities, police, ambulances, schools, and other public property. Some arrests and prosecutions were therefore legitimate.

**Cautions.** HRW’s estimate and classification rely partly on local media and rights-monitoring information. Individual detention files are needed to distinguish lawful prosecution from arbitrary detention.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6; [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html), paragraphs 111–116.

### WEB-4B-004 — Military trials and political engineering

**Claim.** HRCP reported that civilians arrested after the 9 May unrest were placed in military custody and referred for military trials. It also reported that some detained PTI leaders were released after leaving the party, condemning the unrest, or joining a new political organization, and that some were pressured to appear publicly on television.

**Why it matters.** This suggests use of coercive legal and security measures to reshape political competition rather than merely punish violence.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–6, lines 277–311 and 338–344.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the governing coalition and its security partners; direct personal direction by Sharif is not established.

**Complication.** PTI leaders and supporters were implicated in serious attacks on state and military sites. The state had a legitimate interest in prosecution.

**Cautions.** HRCP describes allegations of forced political disassociation and notes uncertainty about detainee numbers and legal procedures. These claims require case-level corroboration.

**Independent corroboration.** [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html); [HRW mass-arrests report](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/05/20/pakistan-mass-arrests-target-political-opposition); [Amnesty International statement](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/asa33/6811/2023/en).

### WEB-4B-005 — Rushed censorship and intelligence legislation

**Claim.** RSF reported that Sharif’s government pushed through amendments to PEMRA shortly before Parliament’s dissolution, along with the Official Secrets Amendment Act. RSF said the measures were adopted without adequate consultation and created substantial risks of censorship and harassment of journalists. HRCP separately reported that the Official Secrets amendments expanded intelligence search powers and the Army Act amendment criminalized online defamation of the armed forces.

**Why it matters.** These were formal institutional changes affecting press freedom, information access, surveillance, and political criticism.

**Strongest source.** “Threats to press freedom in Pakistan from hastily adopted laws ahead of elections,” Reporters Without Borders, August 2023, [direct link](https://rsf.org/en/threats-press-freedom-pakistan-hastily-adopted-laws-ahead-elections), paragraphs 294–318.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for Sharif’s government and Parliament; implementation depended on PEMRA, intelligence agencies, and military authorities.

**Complication.** The government presented the amendments as measures to protect journalists, regulate disinformation, and address cybercrime or espionage.

**Cautions.** RSF is a press-freedom advocacy organization. The precise legal effect should be checked against the enacted statutory texts and subsequent court decisions.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 6–7; [Freedom House Freedom on the Net](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-net/2023).

### WEB-4B-006 — Press freedom and media independence were constrained

**Claim.** RSF described Pakistan’s media environment as one in which political and military elites retained broad control, media regulators favored government interests, and journalists faced administrative and criminal sanctions for crossing implicit red lines. Freedom House also reported interference with distribution and broadcasting, attacks, presenter bans, and journalist disappearances.

**Why it matters.** Independent information is essential for voters to evaluate the government and for civil society to monitor abuse.

**Strongest source.** [RSF Pakistan country profile](https://rsf.org/en/country/pakistan), paragraphs 380–392.

**Period/status.** Broader inherited condition, continuing during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif’s government controlled or influenced federal media regulators and used legal tools, but the military’s role was substantial and longstanding.

**Complication.** Pakistan retained a large and diverse private media sector, including independent Urdu and English-language outlets.

**Cautions.** RSF’s country profile includes current and historical information, not only 2023. The record should be paired with 2023-specific incidents.

**Independent corroboration.** [RSF 2023 laws statement](https://rsf.org/en/threats-press-freedom-pakistan-hastily-adopted-laws-ahead-elections); [Freedom House Freedom in the World](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023).

### WEB-4B-007 — Digital restrictions and internet shutdowns

**Claim.** Freedom House classified Pakistan’s internet as “Not Free” and reported that the government blocked platforms and imposed a four-day internet shutdown in May 2023 to impede expressions of support for Imran Khan. It also reported that journalist Imran Riaz Khan disappeared during the crackdown and returned in September.

**Why it matters.** Digital controls directly restricted political organizing, independent reporting, and opposition communication.

**Strongest source.** *Freedom on the Net 2023: Pakistan*, Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-net/2023), paragraphs 30–40.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for federal telecommunications and security policy. Direct responsibility for the journalist’s disappearance remains unproven.

**Complication.** The government could justify temporary restrictions as a response to incitement and violent attacks during the May crisis.

**Cautions.** Freedom House is advocacy-oriented, and its coverage period runs from June 2022 through May 2023. Later-2023 digital restrictions require additional inspection.

**Independent corroboration.** [Amnesty statement on internet restrictions](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/pakistan-authorities-must-show-restraint-and-lift-internet-restrictions-immediately); [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 6–7.

### WEB-4B-008 — Courts provided some checks but did not secure consistent restraint

**Claim.** Courts allowed peaceful marches that administrators attempted to block, found that Punjab’s caretaker government exceeded its mandate, and the Supreme Court declared military trials of civilians unconstitutional in October. However, another Supreme Court bench suspended that ruling in December, and courts did not prevent the broader political crackdown.

**Why it matters.** The record shows that independent checks existed but were inconsistent and insufficient to protect political freedom during the crisis.

**Strongest source.** *State of Human Rights in 2023*, HRCP, 2024, [PDF](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 5–7, lines 316–344 and 366–374.

**Period/status.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif’s government did not fully control the courts, but its policies were repeatedly subject to judicial challenge.

**Complication.** Judicial intervention demonstrates that Pakistan retained meaningful institutional checks, even during severe political conflict.

**Cautions.** The report summarizes judgments; the underlying court orders should be inspected for precise holdings, procedural posture, and later effect.

**Independent corroboration.** [UK House of Commons Library](https://commonslibrary.parliament.uk/research-briefings/cbp-9883); [President of Pakistan caretaker statement](https://president.gov.pk/news/president-concerned-about-legality-of-caretaker-govts-beyond-90-days-2).

### WEB-4B-009 — Unequal political access for women, minorities, and marginalized groups

**Claim.** Freedom House reported that non-Muslim minorities had reserved legislative representation but little say in selecting reserved-seat representatives; Ahmadis faced separate voter rolls and political discrimination; women faced practical voting restrictions in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa and Balochistan; and the military marginalized ethnic figures associated with suspected anti-state politics.

**Why it matters.** Formal elections did not provide equal political access across all groups.

**Strongest source.** *Freedom in the World 2023: Pakistan*, Freedom House, 2023, [direct link](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023), paragraphs 87–96.

**Period/status.** Inherited conditions continuing during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Low to moderate. Some inequalities were structural and provincial; federal electoral law and security policy nevertheless remained relevant.

**Complication.** Pakistan’s joint electorate system and reserved seats did provide formal representation for non-Muslim minorities, and major ethnic groups were represented in Parliament.

**Cautions.** Freedom House’s political-rights assessment is comparative and not a complete account of all reforms or local variation.

**Independent corroboration.** [HRCP report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 1–8; [FAFEN publications](https://fafen.org/publications).

### WEB-4B-010 — No demonstrated strengthening of democratic resilience

**Claim.** Pakistan ended 2023 with delayed elections, expanded security and media powers, a weakened opposition, military influence over civilian politics, restricted digital communication, and caretaker-governance disputes. Courts and civil society remained active, but no durable institutional reform clearly strengthened political freedom.

**Why it matters.** This is the end-state relevant to whether Sharif left democracy stronger than he inherited it.

**Strongest source.** Cross-source synthesis of [HRCP](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), [Freedom House](https://freedomhouse.org/country/pakistan/freedom-world/2023), [RSF](https://rsf.org/en/threats-press-freedom-pakistan-hastily-adopted-laws-ahead-elections), and [U.S. State Department reporting](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html).

**Period/status.** End-of-period assessment.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif governed only until August, and military, judicial, provincial, and caretaker institutions shared responsibility.

**Complication.** Parliament remained active, courts issued constraining decisions, opposition voters retained meaningful electoral support, and formal multiparty competition survived.

**Cautions.** “Democratic resilience” has no single objective measure. This is a synthesis, not a score.

**Independent corroboration.** [Freedom House analysis of the subsequent election](https://freedomhouse.org/article/pakistan-polls-february-election-results-reflect-unexpected-rejection-status-quo); [HRCP annual report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf).

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **4B.1 — Genuine electoral choice:** **Adverse/mixed.** Pakistan retained multiparty competition, but provincial elections were delayed and PTI faced arrests, censorship, and organizational pressure.
- **4B.2 — No entrenchment of power:** **Adverse evidence.** The government supported or tolerated measures that weakened PTI, expanded security powers, restricted media, and delayed electoral processes. The extent of Sharif’s personal direction remains unclear.
- **4B.3 — Opposition and civic freedom:** **Adverse evidence.** Opposition members, journalists, protesters, and civic groups faced arrests, intimidation, censorship, disappearances, and shutdowns.
- **4B.4 — Independent checks on power:** **Mixed.** Courts and Parliament sometimes constrained the executive, but military influence, rushed legislation, and suspended judicial remedies weakened checks.
- **4B.5 — Politically neutral institutions:** **Adverse/mixed.** HRCP reported political engineering and appointments involving senior military figures. The military’s influence over neutral institutions remained substantial.
- **4B.6 — Independent information and media:** **Adverse evidence.** PEMRA controls, rushed media legislation, journalist intimidation, broadcast restrictions, and internet shutdowns materially reduced independent information.
- **4B.7 — Equal political rights:** **Adverse/mixed.** Women, Ahmadis, non-Muslim minorities, Baloch, Pashtun, and other marginalized groups retained formal rights but faced unequal practical access.
- **4B.8 — Term limits and transfer:** **Adverse/mixed.** Sharif left office when the National Assembly was dissolved, but delayed provincial elections and prolonged caretaker rule weakened constitutional succession.
- **4B.9 — Digital freedom and surveillance:** **Adverse evidence.** Internet shutdowns, platform restrictions, surveillance powers, and online defamation provisions were used or expanded during political conflict.
- **4B.10 — Lasting democratic resilience:** **Unresolved/adverse overall.** Political institutions survived, but no durable strengthening was demonstrated; several institutions and freedoms were weaker by year-end.

## 5. Information environment

Research was difficult because:

- The military and intelligence services had substantial influence over politics, media, and national security.
- PTI and government accounts of the May unrest were sharply polarized.
- Detention lists, disappearance cases, military-court proceedings, and election-administration decisions were not fully transparent.
- Journalists and civil-society monitors faced intimidation, censorship, and restricted access.
- Some sources covered the entire Sharif coalition or longer historical periods rather than only 2023.
- The caretaker period after August complicates attribution of late-2023 restrictions and election administration.
- Courts provided important records, but judgments were sometimes suspended or overtaken by later proceedings.
- The February 2024 election offers retrospective evidence of opposition resilience but should not be treated as direct 2023 outcome evidence.

### C. Inspected but not retained as final evidence

- Amnesty and HRCP pages that were accessible only through limited metadata or failed retrieval were retained as corroborating sources rather than used for precise factual records.
- The 2024 Freedom House election analysis was used only for retrospective corroboration of opposition resilience, not as direct evidence of Sharif’s 2023 conduct.

- [Amnesty Pakistan 2022/23 report](https://www.amnesty.org/en/documents/pol10/5670/2023/en)

## 7. Counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate URLs inspected or attempted: 16.
- Final evidence records: 10.
- Main source families retained: HRCP, Freedom House, RSF, Human Rights Watch, Amnesty International, and the U.S. State Department.
- Remaining gaps: direct court records on election postponement; Election Commission documents; complete statutory texts and legislative debates for PEMRA and Official Secrets amendments; detainee and disappearance lists; appointment records for politically sensitive institutions; and detailed evidence on the federal government’s personal involvement in political engineering.
- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# 5B — Economic Well-Being and Prosperity  
## Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

### 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister until the National Assembly’s dissolution on 9 August 2023; a caretaker administration governed thereafter. His formal authority covered the federal budget, economic policy, cabinet administration, and negotiations with international lenders. His practical authority was constrained by coalition politics, provincial autonomy, the military establishment, external creditors, the IMF, and depleted foreign-exchange reserves. The June 2023 Special Investment Facilitation Council formally included the army chief in economic coordination.

Pakistan entered 2023 with accumulated fiscal and external imbalances, the economic effects of the 2022 floods, high debt, inflation, exchange-rate pressure, and weak reserves. The government secured a nine-month IMF Stand-By Arrangement in July 2023, avoiding an immediate balance-of-payments crisis, but stabilization required fiscal restraint, exchange-rate flexibility, energy-price adjustments, and structural reforms. The short-term outcome was severe: the World Bank estimated FY2023 GDP contraction of 0.6%, average inflation of 29.2%, poverty rising to 39.4%, public debt reaching 82.3% of GDP, and real household incomes being damaged by food, energy, and employment shocks.

The evidence is relatively strong for macroeconomic outcomes and formal policy decisions, but weaker for actual budget execution, distributional incidence, corruption, procurement, and household-level effects. Official statistics were affected by revisions, different fiscal-year conventions, import controls, and limited recent household-survey data.

---

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|

| WEB-5B-02 | imf_sba_stabilization | The government secured a $3 billion, nine-month IMF SBA in July, with substantial policy conditions. | 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.9 |
| WEB-5B-03 | fiscal_consolidation_and_reform_gap | Stabilization narrowed the primary deficit but left large overall deficits and unresolved structural weaknesses. | 5B.3, 5B.4, 5B.6, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-04 | fy24_budget_program | The budget proposed tax, agriculture, IT, SME, export, and youth-finance measures, alongside fiscal consolidation. | 5B.1, 5B.4, 5B.6, 5B.8 |
| WEB-5B-05 | inadequate_poverty_protection | Temporary transfers and subsidies did not fully protect poor households from inflation and flood-related income losses. | 5B.8, 5B.9 |

---

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-5B-01 — FY2023 recession, inflation, poverty, and debt stress

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank estimated that Pakistan’s real GDP contracted by 0.6% in FY2023 after 6.1% growth in FY2022; average inflation reached 29.2%; poverty rose to an estimated 39.4%; public debt including guaranteed debt reached 82.3% of GDP; and private consumption and real household incomes declined. This is the central outcome record for the period.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), Executive Summary, pp. 1–3, especially paragraphs discussing GDP, inflation, poverty, debt, and household income.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns FY2023, overlapping the requested calendar year.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. These were national outcomes during Sharif’s government, but the report identifies inherited imbalances, floods, global commodity prices, global monetary tightening, and political uncertainty as causes. Attribution to Sharif’s discretionary decisions is therefore partial.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The World Bank also records a narrower current-account deficit and a decline in the primary deficit, suggesting some stabilization progress. The government’s own Economic Survey reported different estimates and emphasized recovery measures.

**Cautions.** The poverty estimate was a World Bank projection; the latest suitable Household Integrated Economic Survey was from 2018. GDP and fiscal estimates differ across institutions and fiscal-year definitions.

**Corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–5; [World Bank Pakistan data](https://data.worldbank.org/country/PK).

---

### WEB-5B-02 — IMF Stand-By Arrangement

**Claim and significance.** On 12 July 2023, the IMF approved a nine-month, approximately $3 billion Stand-By Arrangement for Pakistan. The program required fiscal adjustment, exchange-rate flexibility, tighter monetary conditions, energy-sector reform, state-owned-enterprise reform, and climate-related measures. It released immediate financing and helped avert an imminent balance-of-payments crisis.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan: IMF Executive Board Approves US$3 Billion Stand-By Arrangement*, International Monetary Fund, 12 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/07/12/pr23261-pakistan-imf-exec-board-approves-us3bil-sba), opening paragraphs and program description.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the government’s negotiation and formal acceptance of the program; weaker for later implementation because Sharif left office in August and a caretaker government continued the program.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The program reduced immediate default risk but imposed measures likely to worsen short-term household pressures, including energy-price adjustments and fiscal restraint. The World Bank noted that reforms remained incomplete and risks exceptionally high.

**Cautions.** The IMF press release is an institutional account of the approved program and does not independently assess social costs or political feasibility.

**Corroboration.** [World Bank, *Pakistan Development Update*, October 2023](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf); [IMF country-report catalogue](https://www.elibrary.imf.org/view/journals/002/2023/260/002.2023.issue-260-en.xml).

---

### WEB-5B-03 — Stabilization without resolution of structural weaknesses

**Claim and significance.** By FY2023, Pakistan’s primary deficit narrowed to approximately 0.8% of GDP, but the overall fiscal deficit remained about 7.8% of GDP and public debt increased. The World Bank attributed continuing weakness to rigid expenditure, low revenue collection, high interest costs, weak tax administration, state-owned-enterprise problems, and fragmented fiscal institutions.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), Executive Summary, pp. 2–4, and “Restoring Fiscal Sustainability,” pp. 17–28.

**Period fit.** FY2023 and end-of-period assessment in October 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The Sharif government adopted fiscal restraint and the FY2023–24 budget, but many weaknesses predated it and reforms extended beyond its brief tenure.

**Contrary evidence.** IMF approval and a narrower primary deficit indicate that the government did make meaningful short-term stabilization decisions.

**Cautions.** World Bank figures are estimates and differ from government figures. “Reform gap” is an institutional assessment, not proof of personal negligence by Sharif.

**Corroboration.** [IMF, “Pakistan’s Financing Conundrum”](https://blog-pfm.imf.org/en/pfmblog/2023/10/23/pakistan-financing-conundrum); [World Bank, “Pakistan Economic Recovery, Inclusive Growth Require Bold Reforms”](https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/endpovertyinsouthasia/pakistan-economic-recovery-inclusive-growth-require-bold-reforms).

---

### WEB-5B-04 — FY2023–24 budget measures

**Claim and significance.** The federal budget proposed measures aimed at agriculture, information technology, SMEs, exports, and youth finance. It allocated Rs10 billion for small-farmer loans, proposed a Rs5 billion IT venture-capital fund, offered tax concessions for IT and SMEs, and allocated Rs10 billion for subsidized small-business loans through the prime minister’s youth program. An Export Council under the prime minister was also proposed.

**Strongest source.** *Finance Minister’s Speech: Budget 2023–24*, Ministry of Finance, Government of Pakistan, June 2023, [direct link](https://finance.gov.pk/budget/Budget_2023_24/Speech_english_2023_24.pdf), pp. 8–10.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the 2023–24 budget adopted during Sharif’s premiership.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for agenda-setting and cabinet/parliamentary budget responsibility; weaker for implementation and results after August.

**Contrary evidence.** The World Bank found that weak confidence, import controls, high borrowing costs, and structural constraints limited private investment and growth. Announced allocations therefore cannot be treated as delivered outcomes.

**Cautions.** This is a government speech and is strongest evidence of stated policy, not implementation or independent effectiveness.

**Corroboration.** [Associated Press of Pakistan, budget reporting](https://www.app.com.pk/budget/budget-2023-24/federal-budget-2023-24-at-a-glance); [World Bank Pakistan Development Update](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf).

---

### WEB-5B-05 — Protection of poor households was insufficient

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank concluded that record food and energy prices, lower labor incomes, and flood-related crop and livestock losses substantially reduced real household incomes. Temporary increases in cash transfers and a one-time fuel subsidy were insufficient to protect poor and vulnerable households.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), Executive Summary, p. 2.

**Period fit.** FY2023, with effects continuing into calendar 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The government controlled transfer and subsidy policy, but floods, global prices, exchange-rate conditions, and IMF constraints were major external or inherited factors.

**Contrary evidence.** BISP budget expansion and emergency transfers show an attempt at mitigation. The existence of insufficient protection does not establish that no relief was provided.

**Cautions.** The World Bank’s poverty and household-income estimates were partly modeled because recent household survey data were unavailable.

**Corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), pp. 4–5; [BISP 2023–24 budget records](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/MGRjZTI4Y2YtM2UxMy00ODk1LWE3OTktZDlkYjQwMjFiODAw).

---

### WEB-5B-06 — Continuation of BISP social protection

**Claim and significance.** The Benazir Income Support Programme maintained a dedicated 2023–24 budget and administrative infrastructure, including audited financial statements, a national socioeconomic registry, complaint mechanisms, and program components for cash assistance, education, and nutrition. This indicates continuity of a major social-protection institution during the crisis.

**Strongest source.** *Benazir Income Support Programme: Financials and Programme Records*, BISP, accessed for 2023–24 records, [direct link](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/MGRjZTI4Y2YtM2UxMy00ODk1LWE3OTktZDlkYjQwMjFiODAw), financial and reports sections listing the 2023–24 budget and audited statements.

**Period fit.** 2023–24 program period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. BISP is a continuing state institution rather than a program created by Sharif personally. His government approved federal budget support and operated it during the relevant period.

**Contrary evidence.** Administrative continuity does not demonstrate adequate coverage, inflation-indexed benefits, or absence of exclusion and leakage.

**Cautions.** The inspected page is primarily an official catalogue of records; detailed execution and beneficiary-incidence data require separate examination.

**Corroboration.** [Associated Press of Pakistan, reporting on increased BISP allocations](https://www.app.com.pk/budget/budget-2023-24/budgetary-allocations-for-bisp-increased-to-mitigate-sufferings-of-poor-segments); [World Bank PDU](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf).

---

### WEB-5B-07 — Military participation in economic governance

**Claim and significance.** The June 2023 Special Investment Facilitation Council included the army chief and military officials in an investment and economic-coordination structure chaired by the prime minister. This expanded the military’s formal role in economic decision-making and complicates attribution of economic policy exclusively to the civilian cabinet.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Establishes the Special Investment Facilitation Council*, UNCTAD Investment Policy Monitor, 20 June 2023, [direct link](https://investmentpolicy.unctad.org/investment-policy-monitor/measures/4366/pakistan-establishes-the-special-investment-facilitation-council-sifc/), establishment description.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional connection: Sharif’s government established the council and the prime minister chaired it.

**Contrary evidence.** The government described SIFC as an investment facilitation and coordination mechanism intended to reduce administrative barriers, not as a military takeover of economic policy.

**Cautions.** UNCTAD summarizes the formal arrangement but cannot fully measure informal military influence or the council’s actual economic performance.

**Corroboration.** [Pakistan Board of Investment, SIFC description](https://invest.gov.pk/node/1996?language_id=en%3Fomfrpegdxlgtkegd); [SIFC official leadership page](https://sifc.gov.pk/).

---

### WEB-5B-08 — Weakness of household and poverty measurement

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank stated that its FY2022 and FY2023 poverty estimates were projections because the latest Household Integrated Economic Survey suitable for accurate poverty measurement was conducted in 2018. This materially limits claims about distribution, regional incidence, and changes in living standards.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), p. 2, footnote 1.

**Period fit.** Later 2023 assessment of FY2023 conditions.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak to moderate. Data limitations concern the national statistical system and predate Sharif, though the government had responsibility for improving timely measurement.

**Contrary evidence.** Price, GDP, fiscal, and exchange-rate data provide independent evidence of substantial economic stress even without a recent household survey.

**Cautions.** Poverty estimates should not be treated as directly observed headcounts.

**Corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf), which warns that reported figures may not represent the full national picture; [World Bank Pakistan Data](https://data.worldbank.org/country/PK).

---

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **5B.1 — Broad and sustainable prosperity:** Partially documented. The budget and IMF program pursued stabilization and productive sectors, but outcomes were recessionary and poverty increased. Evidence: WEB-5B-01, 02, 04, 05, 06.
- **5B.10 — Lasting economic trajectory:** Unresolved and adverse evidence predominates. The IMF deal prevented immediate crisis, but the World Bank judged the trajectory unsustainable without deeper reforms. Evidence: WEB-5B-01, 02, 03, 08.
- **5B.2 — Qualified economic leadership:** Gap. The record identifies finance ministers and central institutions but does not adequately establish appointment quality, operational independence, or performance-based retention.
- **5B.3 — Macroeconomic stability:** Mixed. The government secured IMF financing and reduced the primary deficit, but inflation, debt, depreciation, reserves, and the overall deficit remained severe. Evidence: WEB-5B-01, 02, 03.
- **5B.4 — Fair economic rules:** Limited evidence. Budget measures supported SMEs, IT, agriculture, and exports, but import controls, exchange-rate restrictions, and state-owned-enterprise distortions impaired predictability. Evidence: WEB-5B-03, 04, 07.
- **5B.5 — Resistance to economic capture:** Major gap. SIFC’s military role raises institutional-capture questions, but the inspected record does not establish specific corruption, procurement abuse, or remedial action attributable to Sharif.
- **5B.6 — Productive public investment:** Mixed and incomplete. Budget allocations and sector initiatives were documented, but execution, quality, and outcomes were not sufficiently inspected. Evidence: WEB-5B-04, 06.
- **5B.7 — Evidence and correction:** Mixed. The government used IMF, World Bank, and fiscal evidence to change course, but data limitations and continued structural weaknesses complicate claims of evidence-based correction. Evidence: WEB-5B-02, 03, 08.
- **5B.8 — Fair distribution:** Adverse outcome evidence is strong; policy-incidence evidence is incomplete. Inflation, food and energy shocks, lower labor income, and flood losses disproportionately harmed poorer households, while BISP provided mitigation. Evidence: WEB-5B-01, 05, 06, 08.
- **5B.9 — Managing economic shocks:** Mixed. The IMF agreement and social-protection measures addressed immediate solvency and poverty risks, but the World Bank judged relief insufficient and recovery fragile. Evidence: WEB-5B-02, 05, 06.

---

## 5. Information environment and evidence limitations

- Pakistan’s fiscal year differs from the calendar year, so FY2023 evidence only partly maps onto January–December 2023.
- GDP, inflation, fiscal, and poverty estimates differ between the government, IMF, World Bank, and independent observers.
- The World Bank explicitly noted that poverty estimates were projections based on an outdated 2018 household survey.
- Government budget documents establish planned measures but not implementation or incidence.
- BISP and government sources are useful for formal allocations and institutional activity but are not independent evaluations.
- The economic crisis overlapped with political instability, import controls, floods, global commodity shocks, and tight international financing. These complicate ruler attribution.
- Military participation in economic institutions is formally documented, but informal influence and commercial benefits require deeper investigation.
- Audit and procurement evidence was not fully extractable from the supplied Auditor-General PDF during this pass.

---

### Accepted final evidence

1. World Bank, *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, October 2023.

5. UNCTAD, SIFC measure page, 20 June 2023.
6. HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*.

### Opened corroboration

- World Bank Pakistan Data.
- World Bank South Asia reform analysis.
- Associated Press of Pakistan budget reports.
- IMF country-report catalogue.
- BISP programme archive.

### Promising leads not fully inspected

- Auditor-General of Pakistan comparative audit report.
- IMF Public Financial Management Blog, “Pakistan’s Financing Conundrum.”
- ADB, “Pakistan Economic Recovery Rests on Resuming Robust Reforms.”
- World Bank April 2023 Pakistan Development Update.
- AP report on the IMF delay.
- Detailed Economic Survey 2022–23.
- BISP audited financial statements and beneficiary-incidence data.
- Academic research on inflation distribution and federal procurement.
- SIFC implementation, project approvals, and conflict-of-interest safeguards.

### Rejected or access-blocked

- The Auditor-General PDF, Economic Survey PDF, IMF PFM blog, and ADB page were identified but not reliably extractable in this pass because of access or document-rendering failures. They remain research leads rather than evidence records.

**Source count:** 8 developed evidence records; 6 principal final sources; 5 additional corroborating sources; 8 material research gaps or uninspected leads.
