--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# 6B — Social Well-Being and Human Development  
## Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

### 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister until 9 August 2023; a caretaker government then administered the country. His federal government controlled national budgets, BISP, federal health and education programs, disaster coordination, and negotiations with international donors. Delivery was heavily shared with provinces, local institutions, international agencies, and established programs. Fiscal crisis, inflation, the 2022 floods, weak public services, and political instability sharply limited what could be delivered during 2023.

The strongest evidence concerns flood-related humanitarian needs and federal social-protection expansion. Evidence is weaker for service quality, actual spending, regional distribution, maternal-health outcomes, and whether programs produced durable improvements. Pakistan’s education, poverty, and health indicators often have substantial time lags and inconsistent survey coverage.

---

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-6B-01 | flood_recovery_social_damage | One year after the floods, millions still lacked safe water, nutrition, schools, and health services. | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-02 | flood_response_delivery | UNICEF and partners delivered substantial health, water, nutrition, psychosocial, and education support, but needs exceeded resources. | 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.5, 6B.6 |
| WEB-6B-03 | bisp_expansion | BISP increased its budget and beneficiary coverage and expanded education, nutrition, disability, and transgender inclusion. | 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.7, 6B.8, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-04 | bisp_payment_transparency | The Benazir Social Protection Account introduced beneficiary-selected bank accounts and direct payments through competitively selected banks. | 6B.2, 6B.5, 6B.7, 6B.8, 6B.9 |
| WEB-6B-05 | education_learning_crisis | Pakistan faced a severe learning crisis; later World Bank–UNESCO evidence records widespread inability to read with comprehension by age ten. | 6B.2, 6B.10 |
| WEB-6B-06 | human_development_data_limits | Available evidence does not permit confident attribution of 2023 changes in national health, education, or poverty outcomes to Sharif. | 6B.5, 6B.10 |

---

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-6B-01 — Flood-related social damage remained severe

**Claim and significance.** On 25 August 2023, UNICEF reported that approximately 8 million people in flood-affected areas still lacked safe water, more than 1.5 million children required lifesaving nutrition interventions, and recovery remained underfunded. The floods had damaged or destroyed approximately 30,000 schools, 2,000 health facilities, and 4,300 water systems.

**Strongest source.** *One year on from catastrophic floods, millions of children in Pakistan still need urgent support*, UNICEF Pakistan, 25 August 2023, [direct link](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/press-releases/one-year-catastrophic-floods-millions-children-pakistan-still-need-urgent-support), paragraphs 30–38.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023 conditions and the continuing effects of the 2022 floods.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The federal government was responsible for national recovery coordination and public services, but the disaster’s scale, provincial implementation, and donor financing were outside Sharif’s sole control.

**Contrary evidence.** UNICEF also reported that government, UNICEF, and partners had reached millions with health, water, nutrition, psychosocial, and education services.

**Cautions.** UNICEF’s figures describe affected populations and humanitarian needs; they do not independently measure federal budget execution or assign responsibility to Sharif.

**Corroboration.** [Associated Press, UNICEF flood follow-up](https://apnews.com/article/a6e12d43c64fa516382083d18dedebcb); [World Bank, children and families after the floods](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099551007112340233/pdf/IDU085b5b1270bae40499408fda0b00c1c3abaf3.pdf).

---

### WEB-6B-02 — Humanitarian delivery occurred but remained inadequate

**Claim and significance.** Between August 2022 and August 2023, UNICEF and partners reached 3.6 million people with primary health care, provided safe water to 1.7 million people, gave psychosocial support to more than 545,000 children and caregivers, supported education for more than 258,000 children, screened 2.1 million children for severe acute malnutrition, and admitted 172,000 children for treatment. UNICEF nevertheless stated that needs exceeded available resources.

**Strongest source.** UNICEF Pakistan, *One year on from catastrophic floods*, 25 August 2023, [direct link](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/press-releases/one-year-catastrophic-floods-millions-children-pakistan-still-need-urgent-support), paragraphs 36–38.

**Period fit.** Covers the ruler’s period and the preceding post-flood response.

**Ruler attribution.** Limited to moderate. These were primarily donor- and agency-supported operations; Sharif’s government enabled national coordination but cannot receive sole credit for delivery.

**Contrary evidence.** The same source records unreached children, underfunding, and continuing service gaps.

**Cautions.** Reach figures are program outputs, not proof of lasting improvements in health, education, nutrition, or household welfare.

**Corroboration.** [UNICEF Pakistan data profile](https://data.unicef.org/country/pak); [UNICEF flood response material](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/).

---

### WEB-6B-03 — BISP expanded coverage and vulnerability targeting

**Claim and significance.** At a 7 July 2023 launch, BISP reported that its budget had increased from Rs235 billion to Rs404.2 billion and coverage from 7.2 million to 9 million beneficiary families. It also reported increases in education stipends, undergraduate scholarships, nutrition support, and a fuel subsidy. BISP stated that it had expanded eligibility for transgender people and lowered the poverty threshold for persons with disabilities.

**Strongest source.** *“Benazir Social Protection Account” is revolutionary milestone in 15 year journey of Benazir Income Support Programme*, Benazir Income Support Programme, 7 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/YzE2MTM2N2UtMDZkZS00MjA5LTlhNWUtYmMxMTQzYzM2Y2Qw), paragraphs 241–254.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong to moderate. Sharif was the chief guest and launched the initiative; the federal government controlled budget approval. BISP itself predates his tenure and had its own administration.

**Contrary evidence.** The announcement does not establish that the expanded benefits kept pace with inflation, reached all eligible households, or produced lasting poverty reduction.

**Cautions.** Most numerical claims come from BISP officials and are not independently audited in the inspected source.

**Corroboration.** [BISP 2023–24 budget and financial archive](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/MGRjZTI4Y2YtM2UxMy00ODk1LWE3OTktZDlkYjQwMjFiODAw); [Associated Press of Pakistan budget report](https://www.app.com.pk/budget/budget-2023-24/budgetary-allocations-for-bisp-increased-to-mitigate-sufferings-of-poor-segments).

---

### WEB-6B-04 — Social Protection Accounts sought more transparent payments

**Claim and significance.** BISP launched “Benazir Social Protection Accounts” in July 2023. The stated design allowed beneficiaries—primarily women—to receive direct payments, choose among participating banks, and withdraw funds at their convenience. Four banks were selected through a competitive process, and the pilot began in several cities and regions.

**Strongest source.** BISP, 7 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/YzE2MTM2N2UtMDZkZS00MjA5LTlhNWUtYmMxMTQzYzM2Y2Qw), paragraphs 241–246 and 254–255.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for formal launch and political sponsorship; moderate for implementation because the scheme was initially a pilot.

**Contrary evidence.** The source does not show whether beneficiaries could actually exercise bank choice, whether payment costs fell, or whether exclusion and fraud declined.

**Cautions.** This is an official program account and should be tested against beneficiary surveys, audit reports, and payment data.

**Corroboration.** [BISP procurement and financial records](https://www.bisp.gov.pk/NewsDetail/MGRjZTI4Y2YtMDZkZS00MjA5LTlhNWUtYmMxMTQzYzM2Y2Qw); [Dawn report on the launch](https://www.dawn.com/news/1763752).

---

### WEB-6B-05 — Pakistan’s education system faced a deep learning crisis

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank–UNESCO Institute for Statistics learning-poverty measure identifies children who cannot read and understand an age-appropriate text by the end of primary school. The Pakistan brief describes learning poverty as unacceptably high, showing that formal enrollment alone did not guarantee meaningful education or improved life chances.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Learning Poverty Brief*, World Bank and UNESCO Institute for Statistics, April 2024, [direct link](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099090524113129017/pdf/P179209-4d233be1-7545-4c70-985a-4a5d15f20e04.pdf), pp. 1–2.

**Period fit.** Later retrospective evidence; it is not a direct 2023 outcome measure.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak. Education is substantially provincial, and the measure reflects long-term conditions predating Sharif.

**Contrary evidence.** Federal and provincial education programs, BISP education stipends, and flood-response schooling support may have mitigated decline in some areas.

**Cautions.** This source should not be used to claim that Sharif caused the learning deficit or that conditions changed during 2023.

**Corroboration.** [UNESCO Global Education Monitoring Report 2023](https://gem-report-2023.unesco.org/monitoring-7); [UNICEF Pakistan education and child data](https://data.unicef.org/country/pak).

---

### WEB-6B-06 — Attribution and measurement limits

**Claim and significance.** The evidence does not support a confident conclusion that national health, education, poverty, or human-development outcomes improved during 2023 because of Sharif’s actions. Most indicators are lagged, several are modeled or donor-produced, and service delivery is divided among federal, provincial, local, and international actors.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Learning Poverty Brief*, World Bank and UNESCO Institute for Statistics, April 2024, [direct link](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099090524113129017/pdf/P179209-4d233be1-7545-4c70-985a-4a5d15f20e04.pdf), methodological discussion; and UNICEF Pakistan, 25 August 2023, [direct link](https://www.unicef.org/pakistan/press-releases/one-year-catastrophic-floods-millions-children-pakistan-still-need-urgent-support).

**Period fit.** Methodological and later-retrospective evidence.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak to moderate. It primarily limits inference rather than establishing a policy outcome.

**Contrary evidence.** Formal initiatives—BISP expansion, social-protection accounts, and flood-response coordination—are clearly documented.

**Cautions.** This is a research limitation, not evidence that the government did nothing.

**Corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf); [UNFPA Pakistan maternal-health program](https://pakistan.unfpa.org/en/publications/unfpa-pakistan-maternal-health-program-2023).

---

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **6B.1 — Welfare as a governing purpose:** Evidence of a real welfare component, especially BISP and flood response, but fiscal crisis and underfunded recovery limited fulfillment. Evidence: WEB-6B-01–04.
- **6B.10 — Lasting life chances:** Cannot be established from 2023 evidence. Flood damage and learning-poverty evidence point to continuing disadvantage. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 05, 06.
- **6B.2 — Access to essential services:** Mixed. BISP and humanitarian delivery expanded access for some, while water, nutrition, schooling, and health needs remained substantial. Evidence: WEB-6B-01–04.
- **6B.3 — Priority for vulnerable groups:** Positive formal evidence exists for women, children, disabled people, transgender people, flood victims, and poor households, but incidence and exclusion require independent verification. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 03, 04.
- **6B.4 — Professional service delivery:** Major gap. The inspected sources do not adequately establish staffing, qualifications, procurement quality, or federal administrative performance.
- **6B.5 — Measurement and correction:** Mixed. BISP maintained registries, complaint systems, and program reports, but independent evaluation and outcome measurement remain insufficient. Evidence: WEB-6B-04, 06.
- **6B.6 — Protection during crises:** Mixed. Humanitarian agencies delivered substantial support, but UNICEF reported severe continuing unmet needs and underfunding. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 02.
- **6B.7 — No political allocation of welfare:** BISP formally described itself as non-political and used a national registry, but independent evidence on political neutrality and exclusion was not established.
- **6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity:** BISP’s beneficiary-choice model and inclusion of transgender and disabled people are favorable formal evidence; practical accessibility and discrimination outcomes remain unverified. Evidence: WEB-6B-03, 04.
- **6B.9 — Durable social institutions:** BISP’s statutory basis, registry, payment systems, and reporting structures indicate institutional durability. The flood response was more donor-dependent and temporary. Evidence: WEB-6B-02–04.

---

## 5. Information environment and limitations

- Health, education, poverty, and nutrition outcomes often have delayed reporting and weak annual comparability.
- The 2022 floods created a major confounder: 2023 outcomes reflected both inherited damage and current government response.
- Federal responsibility was limited by provincial control of much education, health, water, and local administration.
- UNICEF and other international agencies delivered many services, making ruler attribution difficult.
- BISP’s strongest numerical claims were official program claims; independent audits, beneficiary surveys, and regional incidence data require further inspection.
- Later learning-poverty evidence is useful for baseline and trajectory but cannot be treated as a direct 2023 result.
- The absence of accessible execution reports prevents firm conclusions about whether announced social allocations were spent effectively.

---

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. UNICEF Pakistan flood-recovery press release, 25 August 2023.
2. BISP Social Protection Account launch release, 7 July 2023.
3. World Bank–UNESCO Pakistan Learning Poverty Brief.
4. HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*.
5. UNFPA Pakistan maternal-health program page.

### Opened corroboration

- BISP budget and financial archive.
- UNICEF Pakistan country-data profile.
- Associated Press flood follow-up.
- Dawn report on BISP Social Protection Accounts.
- UNESCO Global Education Monitoring Report.

### Promising leads not fully inspected

- BISP audited financial statements for FY2022–23 and FY2023–24.
- BISP Annual Reports on nutrition, education conditional cash transfers, and NSER.
- Pakistan Economic Survey 2022–23.
- Federal Annual Budget Statement and performance-based budget.
- World Bank post-flood household assessment.
- FAO/WFP food-security evidence.
- UNFPA maternal-health outcome data.
- Provincial education and health execution reports.
- Independent research on inflation’s distributional effects.

**Source count:** 6 developed records; 5 principal final sources; 5 corroborating sources; 9 major research gaps or uninspected leads.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# 7B — Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty  
## Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

### 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif was prime minister until August 2023, leading a coalition government whose accountability environment was highly politicized. His personal and family integrity was contested through longstanding money-laundering and corruption cases, but several proceedings produced acquittals, bail decisions, or investigative findings of insufficient evidence. These outcomes do not prove exemplary conduct; they do establish that allegations should not be treated as proven wrongdoing.

The most important evidence inspected shows three things:

1. serious allegations were made against Sharif and close family members;
2. in 2023, NAB reported that a fresh investigation found no corruption or money-laundering offense under the applicable law; and
3. Pakistan’s accountability and procurement systems had formal oversight bodies but significant weaknesses in enforcement, transparency, and independence.

The evidence is therefore stronger for documenting allegations, legal outcomes, and institutional conditions than for proving personal deception, illicit enrichment, nepotism, or obstruction. Country-level corruption indexes are relevant context only and cannot establish Sharif’s personal conduct.

---

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Main finding | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-7B-01 | money_laundering_case_no_finding | NAB reported in 2023 that its fresh investigation found no corruption or money laundering against Sharif and family members. | 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.4, 7B.6, 7B.7 |
| WEB-7B-02 | prior_allegations_and_acquittal | The case involved serious allegations of fake remittances and laundering, but an earlier FIA court had acquitted Sharif and Hamza in 2022. | 7B.1, 7B.4, 7B.6 |
| WEB-7B-03 | accountability_framework_formal_oversight | Pakistan had formal NAB, Auditor-General, Public Accounts Committee, PPRA, and Competition Commission oversight structures. | 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-04 | procurement_transparency_gaps | The IMF found fragmented procurement information, no structured analytical database, and weak enforcement of procurement complaints. | 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-05 | sific_institutional_conflict_risk | The SIFC gave military officials a formal role in investment coordination, creating a conflict-of-interest and transparency issue even without proof of personal enrichment. | 7B.3, 7B.5, 7B.9 |
| WEB-7B-06 | integrity_evidence_limit | No inspected source established that Sharif personally profited from office, directed favoritism, obstructed an investigation, or knowingly made a false statement in 2023. | All lenses, especially 7B.1, 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.7 |

---

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-7B-01 — NAB reported no proven offense in the Sharif-family money-laundering case

**Claim and significance.** In May 2023, NAB told an accountability court that a fresh investigation found no evidence of corruption or money laundering against Prime Minister Shehbaz Sharif, his family members, and other accused persons. The prosecutor stated that no offense under the NAB law had been established.

**Strongest source.** “Shehbaz gets clean chit in money laundering case,” *Dawn*, 11 May 2023, [direct link](https://www.dawn.com/news/1752250), paragraphs 38–45.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023, although the underlying case began earlier.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the fact that the finding concerned Sharif and his close family. It does not prove personal innocence independently of the legal process, nor does it establish that Sharif caused the finding.

**Contrary evidence.** The same report records that NAB had previously alleged fake foreign remittances, foreign pay orders, and laundering through family accounts. Those allegations remained part of the case history.

**Cautions.** This is newspaper reporting of a prosecutor’s submission, not the full court judgment. The finding was made in the context of amendments to the National Accountability Ordinance, whose legal relevance the judge required the prosecutor to explain.

**Corroboration.** “Notices to NAB on acquittal pleas of Shehbaz, Hamza,” *Dawn*, 6 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.dawn.com/news/1763252), paragraphs 35–43.

---

### WEB-7B-02 — Serious allegations existed, but earlier proceedings also produced acquittal

**Claim and significance.** The money-laundering case involved allegations that family members and alleged benamidars received fake foreign remittances and that billions of rupees were moved through foreign pay orders into family accounts. However, *Dawn* reported that a special court acquitted Shehbaz and Hamza in October 2022 in the FIA’s Rs16 billion case, and that the court found no probability of conviction on the evidence then available.

**Strongest source.** “Shehbaz gets clean chit in money laundering case,” *Dawn*, 11 May 2023, [direct link](https://www.dawn.com/news/1752250), paragraphs 54–68.

**Period fit.** Later retrospective evidence used to interpret the 2023 proceedings; the underlying allegations and acquittal predate 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong as to the fact that the allegations concerned Sharif and his family; no evidence inspected establishes that Sharif personally received illicit funds.

**Contrary evidence.** NAB’s earlier allegations and the fact that Sharif and family members were indicted in the reference show that the matter was not a trivial or purely invented controversy.

**Cautions.** Acquittal and lack of evidence are not the same as an affirmative finding that every factual allegation was false. The report does not provide the complete judicial record.

**Corroboration.** “Notices to NAB on acquittal pleas of Shehbaz, Hamza,” *Dawn*, 6 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.dawn.com/news/1763252), paragraphs 41–56.

---

### WEB-7B-03 — Formal accountability and procurement institutions existed

**Claim and significance.** Pakistan’s public-investment and procurement system formally assigned oversight roles to NAB, the Auditor-General, the Public Accounts Committee, the Public Procurement Regulatory Authority, and the Competition Commission. These institutions created potential routes for investigation, audit, complaint review, and accountability.

**Strongest source.** *Technical Assistance Report—Public Investment Management Assessment and Climate PIMA: Pakistan*, IMF Country Report No. 23/370, International Monetary Fund, August 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/CR/2023/English/1PAKEA2023002.ashx), pp. 44, 71.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the institutional framework operating during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak to moderate. These were national institutions inherited by Sharif’s government, although the government operated and amended the applicable framework during the period.

**Contrary evidence.** The IMF report simultaneously identified fragmented information, weak enforcement, and capacity problems, limiting the practical independence and effectiveness of the formal structures.

**Cautions.** The report evaluates public-investment management, not Sharif’s personal integrity. Formal institutional existence cannot be equated with effective investigation.

**Corroboration.** [Auditor-General of Pakistan audit repository](https://www.agp.gov.pk/AuditReports); [Pakistan Public Procurement Regulatory Authority](https://www.ppra.org.pk/).

---

### WEB-7B-04 — Procurement oversight was fragmented and weakly enforced

**Claim and significance.** The IMF reported that Pakistan lacked a structured procurement database capable of analyzing information across advertisements, evaluation reports, contract awards, and audit observations. It also stated that procurement complaints were not rigorously enforced and that no structured data existed on compliance and performance.

**Strongest source.** *Technical Assistance Report—Public Investment Management Assessment and Climate PIMA*, IMF, August 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/CR/2023/English/1PAKEA2023002.ashx), pp. 44–46.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns institutional conditions in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak to moderate. The findings concern the state system rather than a specific order by Sharif. They are relevant to whether his government maintained or corrected integrity safeguards, but do not prove personal favoritism.

**Contrary evidence.** The IMF also found that Pakistan had procurement rules, annual Auditor-General review, PPRA complaint procedures, and Competition Commission powers.

**Cautions.** This is a technical institutional assessment, not an investigation into a particular contract or Sharif-family benefit.

**Corroboration.** [Auditor-General of Pakistan](https://www.agp.gov.pk/AuditReports); [PPRA](https://www.ppra.org.pk/).

---

### WEB-7B-05 — SIFC created institutional conflict and transparency risks

**Claim and significance.** The Special Investment Facilitation Council, established in June 2023, included the army chief and military officials in economic and investment coordination under a council chaired by the prime minister. This created a significant public-integrity question because military officials participated in decisions involving investment facilitation, state assets, and economic access.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Establishes the Special Investment Facilitation Council*, UNCTAD Investment Policy Monitor, 20 June 2023, [direct link](https://investmentpolicy.unctad.org/investment-policy-monitor/measures/4366/pakistan-establishes-the-special-investment-facilitation-council-sifc/), establishment description.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution: Sharif’s government established the body and the prime minister chaired it.

**Contrary evidence.** The government presented SIFC as an investment-facilitation mechanism intended to reduce bureaucratic delays and attract foreign investment. No inspected source established that Sharif or his family personally profited from SIFC decisions.

**Cautions.** The source records institutional design but not actual projects, beneficiaries, recusals, asset disclosures, or conflicts. The integrity concern is therefore a risk and transparency issue, not a proven offense.

**Corroboration.** [Pakistan Board of Investment SIFC description](https://invest.gov.pk/node/1996?language_id=en%3Fomfrpegdxlgtkegd); [SIFC official site](https://sifc.gov.pk/).

---

### WEB-7B-06 — No inspected source proved personal enrichment or deliberate deception

**Claim and significance.** The inspected evidence does not establish that Sharif personally profited from public office, knowingly made a false statement in a formal proceeding, obstructed an investigation, directed procurement favoritism, or appointed officials solely to protect family interests during 2023.

**Strongest source.** This is a bounded evidence assessment based on the inspected legal and institutional records: [Dawn, 11 May 2023](https://www.dawn.com/news/1752250); [Dawn, 6 July 2023](https://www.dawn.com/news/1763252); [IMF PIMA report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/CR/2023/English/1PAKEA2023002.ashx).

**Period fit.** Target period, with inherited allegations and later legal developments used as context.

**Ruler attribution.** Not applicable as a positive finding; this record defines the evidentiary limit.

**Contrary evidence.** Serious allegations, indictments, family business connections, and politicized accountability processes warrant further investigation. Absence of a proven finding in the inspected material is not proof of exemplary integrity.

**Cautions.** This is not a claim that misconduct did not occur. It is a statement that the inspected sources did not establish it to the required evidentiary standard.

**Corroboration.** Further court files, asset declarations, procurement records, and IMF governance material are needed.

---

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **7B.1 — Truthfulness:** No verified 2023 false statement by Sharif was established. Allegations and political rhetoric should not be treated as proof. Evidence: WEB-7B-01, 02, 06.
- **7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors:** Gap. The inspected record does not show a clear instance in which Sharif admitted and corrected a proven falsehood or refused correction.
- **7B.3 — Conflicts of interest:** Mixed and incomplete. The SIFC raises institutional conflict concerns, but no personal recusal, disclosure, or family-benefit finding was established. Evidence: WEB-7B-05.
- **7B.4 — Personal profit from office:** No proven finding in the inspected evidence. Serious allegations existed, but NAB and prior court proceedings reported insufficient evidence or acquittal. Evidence: WEB-7B-01, 02, 06.
- **7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments:** Gap. The cabinet and family-political network require investigation, but no appointment record inspected here proves improper selection for personal protection or enrichment.
- **7B.6 — Independent investigation:** Mixed. NAB, FIA, courts, Auditor-General, PPRA, and the Competition Commission existed and acted, but the cases were politically contested and the institutional framework had weaknesses. Evidence: WEB-7B-01, 03, 04.
- **7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment:** Not established. The inspected sources do not prove that Sharif obstructed the cases; the legal changes and government influence over accountability institutions warrant further review.
- **7B.8 — Promises and good faith:** Gap. Formal economic and governance commitments were not compared systematically with implementation in this pass.
- **7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism:** Risk documented, proof absent. SIFC and fragmented procurement systems create opportunities for favoritism, but no specific Sharif-directed transaction was established. Evidence: WEB-7B-04, 05.

---

## 5. Information environment and limitations

- Sharif-family accountability cases were highly politicized, with competing claims that prosecutions were either genuine anti-corruption efforts or politically motivated.
- Media reports often summarized pleadings, prosecutor submissions, or allegations rather than final judgments.
- A “clean chit,” acquittal, or finding of insufficient evidence does not establish that every allegation was false; it establishes the legal evidentiary outcome reported.
- Formal accountability institutions existed, but the IMF identified fragmented information, weak enforcement, and limited analytical capacity.
- Public records concerning beneficial ownership, procurement beneficiaries, asset declarations, and conflicts of interest were not fully inspected.
- Country-level corruption indicators cannot be attributed to Sharif personally.
- The 2023 transition from elected coalition government to caretaker administration complicates responsibility for later institutional decisions.

---

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. *Dawn*, “Shehbaz gets clean chit in money laundering case,” 11 May 2023.
2. *Dawn*, “Notices to NAB on acquittal pleas of Shehbaz, Hamza,” 6 July 2023.
3. IMF, *Technical Assistance Report—Public Investment Management Assessment and Climate PIMA*, August 2023.
4. UNCTAD, SIFC measure page, 20 June 2023.

### Opened corroboration

- Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2023: Pakistan*.
- Auditor-General of Pakistan audit repository.
- Pakistan PPRA website.
- Pakistan Board of Investment SIFC material.

### Promising leads not fully inspected

- IMF Governance and Corruption Diagnostic / PIM legal framework.
- Full court judgments in the FIA and NAB cases.
- NAB supplementary reference and original charge sheets.
- 2023 amendments or ordinances affecting the National Accountability Ordinance.
- Sharif and family asset declarations.
- Federal procurement and SIFC project-level records.
- Appointment records for cabinet, SOE, and investment bodies.
- Parliamentary debates and Public Accounts Committee records.
- Investigative reporting on family business interests and state contracts.

### Blocked or rejected sources

Several APP, Dawn, IMF, and academic links were not reliably retrievable or yielded insufficient underlying documentation in this pass. They remain leads, not accepted evidence.

**Source count:** 6 developed records; 4 principal final sources; 4 corroborating sources; 9 major research gaps or uninspected leads.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence  
## Shehbaz Sharif, Pakistan, 2023

### 1. Orientation

Shehbaz Sharif governed as prime minister until 9 August 2023; a caretaker government then completed the year. His main observable program was crisis stabilization: avoid default, restore foreign-exchange access, implement an IMF-supported budget, protect social spending, revive agriculture and exports, and attract investment through the Special Investment Facilitation Council.

The government achieved a major short-term objective by securing the IMF’s approximately $3 billion Stand-By Arrangement in July 2023, which helped avert an immediate balance-of-payments crisis. It also enacted a budget with agriculture, IT, SME, export, youth, and reconstruction initiatives. However, implementation and outcomes remained weak: FY2023 GDP contracted, inflation and poverty rose, reserves remained critically low, and the World Bank judged that major structural reforms were still outstanding.

Effectiveness is difficult to attribute exclusively to Sharif. The period was shaped by inherited debt and external imbalances, the 2022 floods, global commodity and financing shocks, coalition constraints, military influence, provincial authority, and the short tenure followed by caretaker rule.

---

## 2. Evidence index

| WEB-8B-02 | budget_program_conversion | The FY2023–24 budget translated priorities into agricultural, IT, SME, export, youth, and reconstruction measures. | 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.6 |
| WEB-8B-03 | adverse_macro_outcomes | Despite stabilization, GDP contracted, inflation and poverty rose, reserves fell, and the currency depreciated. | 8B.7, 8B.9, 8B.10 |
| WEB-8B-04 | reform_and_implementation_gap | The World Bank found that major structural constraints, weak investment, and fiscal risks remained unresolved. | 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.8, 8B.10 |

| WEB-8B-07 | transition_and_authority_limits | The elected government ended in August and elections were delayed, limiting the time available for durable implementation. | 8B.5, 8B.9, 8B.10 |

---

## 3. Evidence records

### WEB-8B-01 — Stabilization program and IMF agreement

**Claim and significance.** The government’s clearest 2023 objective was economic stabilization. On 12 July 2023, the IMF approved a nine-month, approximately $3 billion Stand-By Arrangement. The program supported fiscal adjustment, a market-determined exchange rate, tight monetary policy, energy reform, state-owned-enterprise reform, climate resilience, and protection of critical social spending. The IMF and World Bank both assessed that the agreement helped avert an immediate balance-of-payments crisis.

**Strongest source.** *IMF Executive Board Approves US$3 billion Stand-By Arrangement for Pakistan*, International Monetary Fund, 12 July 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/07/12/pr23261-pakistan-imf-exec-board-approves-us3bil-sba), paragraphs 210–226.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for negotiation, policy commitment, and formal acceptance by Sharif’s government; later implementation was shared with the caretaker administration.

**Contrary evidence.** The World Bank reported that the economy remained on an unsustainable trajectory without much broader reforms, and that stabilization imposed substantial short-term costs.

**Cautions.** IMF approval establishes a formal policy achievement, not successful completion of the program or long-term recovery.

**Corroboration.** [World Bank, *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), pp. 1–4.

---

### WEB-8B-02 — The budget converted goals into concrete measures

**Claim and significance.** The FY2023–24 budget translated the government’s stated growth strategy into specific measures: increased agricultural lending, solarization of agricultural tube wells, farmer and agro-industry loans, IT tax concessions, a venture-capital fund, IT graduate training, SME loans, export facilitation, and a prime minister-led Export Council. The budget also described flood reconstruction schemes and a target of moving from stabilization toward growth.

**Strongest source.** *Finance Minister’s Speech: Budget 2023–24*, Ministry of Finance, Government of Pakistan, June 2023, [direct link](https://finance.gov.pk/budget/Budget_2023_24/Speech_english_2023_24.pdf), pp. 6–10.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the budget adopted during Sharif’s premiership.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for formal agenda-setting, cabinet responsibility, and budget sponsorship; weaker for implementation after August.

**Contrary evidence.** The World Bank found that import controls, weak confidence, high borrowing costs, and political uncertainty reduced investment and industrial activity.

**Cautions.** The budget speech establishes intended measures and allocations, not execution, quality, or outcomes. Several claims in the speech, such as completed rehabilitation and economic impact, are government assertions.

**Corroboration.** [Federal Budget 2023–24 portal](https://finance.gov.pk/fb_2023_24.html); [Associated Press of Pakistan budget summary](https://www.app.com.pk/budget/budget-2023-24/federal-budget-2023-24-at-a-glance).

---

### WEB-8B-03 — Stabilization did not produce favorable short-term macroeconomic outcomes

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank estimated that FY2023 real GDP contracted by 0.6% after 6.1% growth in FY2022; average inflation reached 29.2%; poverty rose to an estimated 39.4%; reserves fell to approximately one month of imports; and the rupee depreciated by 28.6% against the dollar. These outcomes show that formal crisis management did not translate into broad short-term economic improvement.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), Executive Summary, pp. 1–3.

**Period fit.** FY2023, assessed in October 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Results occurred under Sharif’s government but reflected inherited imbalances, floods, global prices, global financing conditions, policy choices, and the transition to caretaker rule.

**Contrary evidence.** The current-account deficit and primary deficit narrowed, and the IMF agreement prevented immediate default-type crisis.

**Cautions.** Poverty figures were projections because the latest suitable household survey was from 2018. GDP estimates differed between the government, IMF, and World Bank.

**Corroboration.** [IMF SBA press release](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/07/12/pr23261-pakistan-imf-exec-board-approves-us3bil-sba); [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf).

---

### WEB-8B-04 — Structural reform and implementation gaps remained

**Claim and significance.** The World Bank judged that Pakistan still required major reforms to broaden the tax base, improve expenditure quality, reform state-owned enterprises, reduce energy-sector inefficiency, restore private-sector confidence, and manage debt. It warned that investment would remain low and growth would remain weak without implementation beyond the IMF program.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Development Update: Restoring Fiscal Sustainability*, World Bank, October 2023, [direct link](https://thedocs.worldbank.org/en/doc/cfd9113f24c548efdc86dba482a5e2cf-0310062023/original/Pakistan-Development-Update-October-2023.pdf), Executive Summary, pp. 1–4 and “Roadmap for Addressing Fiscal Constraints,” pp. 26–28.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the end-of-period assessment in 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Sharif’s government was responsible for policy direction and implementation during the elected period, but many structural problems predated it and required longer than sixteen months.

**Contrary evidence.** The IMF agreement and FY2023–24 budget demonstrate that the government adopted at least some reform machinery.

**Cautions.** The World Bank’s assessment is a policy diagnosis, not a personal assessment of Sharif’s competence.

**Corroboration.** [IMF PIMA technical report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/CR/2023/English/1PAKEA2023002.ashx); [World Bank reform analysis](https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/endpovertyinsouthasia/pakistan-economic-recovery-inclusive-growth-require-bold-reforms).

---

### WEB-8B-05 — Economic governance was shared with the military

**Claim and significance.** The Special Investment Facilitation Council, established in June 2023, gave the army chief and military officials formal roles in investment and economic coordination under a council chaired by the prime minister. This expanded the machinery available to the government for investment mobilization but also complicates attribution, civilian control, and accountability.

**Strongest source.** *Pakistan Establishes the Special Investment Facilitation Council*, UNCTAD Investment Policy Monitor, 20 June 2023, [direct link](https://investmentpolicy.unctad.org/investment-policy-monitor/measures/4366/pakistan-establishes-the-special-investment-facilitation-council-sifc/), establishment description.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong institutional attribution: Sharif’s government established the council and the prime minister chaired it.

**Contrary evidence.** The government presented SIFC as a coordination mechanism designed to reduce red tape and attract investment.

**Cautions.** The inspected record does not establish whether SIFC produced successful investments or whether its military participation improved or harmed implementation.

**Corroboration.** [Pakistan Board of Investment SIFC description](https://invest.gov.pk/node/1996?language_id=en%3Fomfrpegdxlgtkegd); [SIFC official site](https://sifc.gov.pk/).

---

### WEB-8B-06 — Public-investment implementation capacity was weak

**Claim and significance.** An IMF technical assessment found that Pakistan’s public-investment program was unaffordable relative to available allocations, that funding for ongoing projects was insufficient, and that underfunding slowed project delivery. It also identified reliance on supplementary grants and virements, weak project selection criteria, incomplete ex-post evaluation, and fragmented oversight.

**Strongest source.** *Technical Assistance Report—Public Investment Management Assessment and Climate PIMA*, IMF Country Report No. 23/370, August 2023, [direct link](https://www.imf.org/-/media/Files/Publications/CR/2023/English/1PAKEA2023002.ashx), pp. 7–9 and 35–48.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns institutional performance during 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for the government’s ability to manage the investment program; weak for personal attribution because the weaknesses were systemic and predated Sharif.

**Contrary evidence.** The IMF found that Pakistan had sound formal project-management and monitoring procedures on paper, including implementation plans and external auditing.

**Cautions.** This is a technical assessment of the state system, not a record of Sharif personally directing a specific failed project.

**Corroboration.** [Auditor-General of Pakistan audit repository](https://www.agp.gov.pk/AuditReports); [Pakistan Planning Commission](https://www.pc.gov.pk/).

---

### WEB-8B-07 — The short elected tenure limited durable achievement

**Claim and significance.** The elected government ended with dissolution of the National Assembly on 9 August 2023, after which a caretaker government administered the country pending delayed elections. This limited the time available to complete the budget, IMF program, investment projects, and structural reforms and makes end-of-year attribution difficult.

**Strongest source.** *Update on the Performance of Democratic Institutions: September 2023*, PILDAT, October 2023, [direct link](https://pildat.org/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/Update-September-2023.pdf), pp. 3–6, including discussion of the caretaker government and election timeline.

**Period fit.** Directly concerns the transition affecting the second half of 2023.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the formal end of Sharif’s government; weaker for responsibility for the caretaker’s subsequent policies.

**Contrary evidence.** The government had already secured the IMF agreement and enacted the FY2023–24 budget before leaving office.

**Cautions.** The transition was constitutionally structured but politically contested; the source is a civil-society monitoring report rather than an official legal record.

**Corroboration.** [HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/wp-content/uploads/2020/09/2024-State-of-human-rights-in-2023-EN.pdf); [UK House of Commons Library, Pakistan political developments](https://commonslibrary.parliament.uk/research-briefings/cbp-9883).

---

## 4. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 — Clear governing program:** Substantially documented. Stabilization, IMF compliance, agricultural recovery, investment, exports, IT, SMEs, and social protection were stated or formalized. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 02.
- **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery:** Mixed to positive. The IMF program, budget, SIFC, and sector allocations were concrete; implementation capacity was weaker. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 02, 05, 06.
- **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system:** Mixed. The government mobilized Parliament, the IMF, cabinet, and military-linked SIFC, but coalition and institutional fragmentation limited unified execution. Evidence: WEB-8B-05, 06, 07.
- **8B.4 — Capable implementers:** Gap. The record identifies economic and administrative institutions but does not adequately test appointments, retention, competence, or replacement decisions.
- **8B.5 — Coordination and control:** Weak to mixed. Formal coordination mechanisms existed, but underfunded projects, fragmented fiscal institutions, provincial authority, and the caretaker transition limited follow-through. Evidence: WEB-8B-05, 06, 07.
- **8B.6 — From plans to practice:** Mixed. The IMF agreement and some program measures were implemented; many budget and structural reforms remained plans or incomplete processes. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 02, 04, 06.
- **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals:** Mixed to adverse. The immediate stabilization goal was partially achieved, but growth, inflation, poverty, reserves, and investment outcomes moved poorly. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 03, 04.
- **8B.8 — Learning and correction:** Some evidence of course correction through the IMF agreement and exchange-rate reform, but no adequate record of systematic audit-based personnel replacement or program correction. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 04, 06.
- **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance:** Mixed. The government managed to secure external financing amid severe crisis, but political conflict, floods, fiscal pressure, and institutional resistance remained unresolved. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 03, 05, 07.
- **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement:** Limited evidence of durable achievement by the end of 2023. The IMF agreement was an important short-term institutional anchor, but the World Bank found that the economic trajectory remained fragile and reform-dependent. Evidence: WEB-8B-01, 04, 07.

---

## 5. Information environment and limitations

- Fiscal-year data and calendar-year political periods do not align.
- GDP, poverty, fiscal, and reserve estimates differ among the government, IMF, and World Bank.
- Poverty estimates were projections based on an outdated household survey.
- Budget speeches establish intentions and allocations, not actual execution.
- Many economic reforms required provincial cooperation, military participation, or a caretaker government.
- Public-investment weaknesses were systemic and cannot automatically be attributed to Sharif personally.
- The period was too short to judge the durability of most investment, IT, agricultural, and SOE reforms.
- Government claims of recovery and completed reconstruction require independent project-level verification.

---

## 6. Source groups

### Accepted final evidence

1. IMF, Pakistan SBA press release, 12 July 2023.
2. World Bank, *Pakistan Development Update*, October 2023.
3. Government of Pakistan, *Budget 2023–24 Finance Minister’s Speech*.
4. IMF, *PIMA and Climate PIMA*, August 2023.
5. PILDAT, *Update on the Performance of Democratic Institutions: September 2023*.

### Opened corroboration

- HRCP, *State of Human Rights in 2023*.
- Federal Budget 2023–24 portal.
- Freedom House retrospective election analysis.
- Pakistan Planning Commission materials.

### Promising leads not fully inspected

- IMF first review under the SBA.
- Detailed Economic Survey 2022–23.
- Auditor-General execution and project audits.
- PILDAT August 2023 update.
- National Assembly questions and budget documents.
- SIFC project approvals and investment outcomes.
- Cabinet implementation reports.
- Provincial development-program execution reports.
- Academic work on flood impacts and inflation distribution.

### Blocked or rejected sources

Several APP, World Bank, and PILDAT links were inaccessible or only partially rendered. They remain leads rather than accepted evidence.

**Source count:** 7 developed evidence records; 5 principal final sources; 4 corroborating sources; 9 major research gaps or uninspected leads.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json`: the workspace filesystem was unavailable in this continuation (`bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR`). I therefore preserved the new records below with globally unique IDs, but they still require manifest insertion by the parent workflow.

## Review-round findings

### WEB-R1-001 — Nuclear command authority is formally centralized in the National Command Authority

Pakistan’s nuclear decision structure places formal authority in the National Command Authority, chaired by the prime minister, while operational expertise and implementation are associated with military institutions. This supports a distinction between Sharif’s formal chairmanship and his uncertain independent operational control.

**Source.** [National Command Authority, Pakistan — reference overview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Command_Authority_%28Pakistan%29), institutional-history section.

**Assessment.** Context only. The source is secondary and Wikipedia-based; a primary National Command Authority Act or official government publication remains necessary. Attribution to Sharif is formal but not sufficient to establish personal control over nuclear operations.

**Lenses:** 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.8.

### WEB-R1-002 — UNMOGIP recorded and monitored the 2023 Line of Control ceasefire environment

The UN mission’s mandate covers the Line of Control and includes investigating and reporting alleged ceasefire violations. A UN report states that during 2023 UNMOGIP continued supervision of the ceasefire and conducted operational field tasks on both sides of the Line of Control.

**Source.** [UNMOGIP mandate](https://unmogip.unmissions.org/en/about-unmogip); [UN document on UNMOGIP activities](https://documents.un.org/api/symbol/access?l=en&s=A%2F79%2F6%28Sect.5%29&t=pdf).

**Assessment.** Strong evidence of an international monitoring mechanism and continued ceasefire-management environment, but not proof that Sharif personally directed restraint. The record does not establish whether Pakistan cooperated fully with every investigation.

**Lenses:** 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.9, 2B.10.

### WEB-R1-003 — Pakistan’s Supreme Court initially invalidated military trials of civilians after the May 2023 unrest

On 23 October 2023, Pakistan’s Supreme Court declared the military trials of civilians arrested in connection with the May 9 violence unlawful and directed that the accused be tried in ordinary criminal courts. In December, the Court suspended the effect of that ruling and allowed the trials to resume pending further proceedings.

**Sources.** [Associated Press of Pakistan, 23 October 2023](https://www.app.com.pk/national/sc-declares-civilians-trial-in-army-courts-as-null-and-void/); [Supreme Court judgment](https://www.supremecourt.gov.pk/downloads_judgements/const.p._24_2023_f.pdf); [Supreme Court suspension, 13 December 2023](https://www.radio.gov.pk/13-12-2023/sc-suspends-ruling-nullifying-civilians-trials-in-military-courts).

**Assessment.** Strong evidence of judicial checking and institutional conflict. The federal government and caretaker authorities supported continuation of military trials, but the sources do not isolate Sharif’s personal direction before August.

**Lenses:** 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9; 3B.1, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8; 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.9.

### WEB-R1-004 — Independent reporting documented enforced-disappearance allegations in Balochistan

The U.S. State Department’s 2023 human-rights report described credible reports of enforced disappearance and alleged that security forces in Balochistan continued to disappear activists, politicians, teachers, and other civilians. Pakistani and Baloch civil-society organizations reported substantially higher numbers, but those counts are not independently verifiable.

**Sources.** [U.S. State Department 2023 Pakistan report via ecoi.net](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107764.html); [Paank Annual Report 2023](https://paank.org/paank-annual-report-2023/); [HRCP Balochistan fact-finding report](https://hrcp-web.org/hrcpweb/hrcp-launches-fact-finding-report-on-rights-violations-in-balochistan/).

**Assessment.** Strong evidence that disappearance allegations were a major 2023 human-rights issue. Attribution to Sharif is high-risk: security operations were shared among federal, military, and provincial institutions, and no inspected source establishes a direct prime-ministerial order.

**Lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.2, 3B.4, 3B.6, 3B.9; 4B.3, 4B.4.

### WEB-R1-005 — The state’s position on military trials was contested by courts and rights organizations

Amnesty International warned in May 2023 that civilians should not be tried under military laws after authorities announced plans to prosecute protesters under the Army Act and Official Secrets Act. The later Supreme Court litigation confirmed that the legality of this policy was contested within Pakistan’s institutions.

**Source.** [Amnesty International, “Pakistan: Civilians must not be tried under military laws”](https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2023/05/pakistan-civilians-must-not-be-tried-under-military-laws).

**Assessment.** Amnesty is an advocacy source, but its claim concerns a publicly documented legal policy and is corroborated by the later Supreme Court proceedings. It does not establish Sharif’s personal responsibility for each prosecution.

**Lenses:** 3B.1, 3B.4, 3B.5, 3B.8; 4B.3, 4B.4, 4B.9.

### WEB-R1-006 — The IMF program provided a formal stabilization anchor but required continued implementation

The IMF approved Pakistan’s nine-month $3 billion Stand-By Arrangement on 12 July 2023. The program required fiscal discipline, exchange-rate flexibility, tight monetary policy, energy reform, SOE governance, and protection of social spending. The IMF explicitly stated that consistent implementation would be critical.

**Source.** [IMF press release, 12 July 2023](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2023/07/12/pr23261-pakistan-imf-exec-board-approves-us3bil-sba), paragraphs 210–226.

**Assessment.** Strong formal evidence for 5B and 8B. It documents a concrete government achievement and program machinery, but not completed implementation or durable success.

**Lenses:** 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.7, 5B.9; 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.6, 8B.9.

---

## Updated gap assessment

- **1B:** Improved on formal authority, but the primary National Command Authority statute and independent evidence on military arsenal safety remain unavailable.
- **2B:** Improved on primary UNMOGIP material and ceasefire monitoring. Independent evidence on procurement, counterterrorism conduct, and caretaker-period deportation policy remains incomplete.
- **3B:** Improved on primary military-court litigation and a stronger international source on disappearances. Direct command attribution and regional case files remain unresolved.
- **4B:** Improved on judicial review of military trials and the institutional limits on executive power. Direct Sharif-government orders concerning censorship and political coercion remain missing.
- **5B:** The IMF source materially strengthens the stabilization record, but BISP execution, SIFC governance, and distributional outcomes remain open.
- **6B:** No new independent service-delivery or beneficiary-level evidence was recoverable in this continuation.
- **7B:** No new primary asset, beneficial-ownership, or complete court records were recoverable. Personal attribution remains the principal gap.
- **8B:** The IMF record strengthens formal-program evidence; project-level execution and durable outcomes remain insufficiently documented.

--- SUPERVISOR RESEARCH TAKEOVER ROUND 2 ---

Research continuation completed for selected chapter 1B.

New defensible evidence recovered:

- `WEB-R2-001` — Pakistan’s National Command Authority Act formally makes the prime minister chair of the NCA, clarifying formal responsibility while leaving operational control institutionally distributed. Source: [National Assembly NCA Act PDF](https://www.na.gov.pk/uploads/documents/1300934560_193.pdf), opening provisions.
- `WEB-R2-002` — Pakistan’s 2023 CERT Rules created national, sectoral, government, critical-infrastructure, federal, and provincial cybersecurity structures, including incident response and critical-information-infrastructure protection. Source: [Associated Press of Pakistan, 16 July 2023](https://www.app.com.pk/national/cabinet-approves-computer-emergency-response-teams-certs-rule-2023-amin/), paragraphs 154–173. Links to 1B.9.
- `WEB-R2-003` — Independent scholarly assessment found Pakistan’s civilian nuclear-security regime broadly consistent with international principles, while identifying transparency, cybersecurity, and nuclear-security-culture weaknesses. Source: [Journal of Strategic Security article](https://digitalcommons.usf.edu/jss/vol16/iss4/15/), abstract and article record. Links to 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.9.
- `WEB-R2-004` — PNRA’s official site lists a dedicated 2023 annual report, but the report itself timed out during retrieval. Source: [PNRA reports index](https://www.pnra.org/report.html). This remains a retrieval lead, not substantive evidence.

Interpretive update:

- The 1B.9 gap is narrowed: a formal 2023 cybersecurity governance framework existed, although it was not specifically framed as catastrophic AI, biological, or nuclear dual-use governance.
- The formal-authority gap is materially improved by the primary NCA Act.
- Independent evidence supports both positive institutional capacity and continuing transparency/cybersecurity weaknesses.
- No evidence was found of a Sharif-specific nuclear posture change, arsenal-safety incident, or personal operational decision.

The local workspace remains unavailable due to `bwrap: loopback: Failed RTM_NEWADDR: Operation not permitted`. Therefore I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json`; the new IDs must be inserted by the parent workflow. No score was assigned.
